Derivation of renormalized Hartree-Fock-Bogoliubov and quantum Boltzmann equations in an interacting Bose gas
Thomas Chen, Michael Hott

Abstract
. Our previous work [ 37 ] presented a rigorous derivation of quantum Boltzmann equations near a Bose-Einstein condensate (BEC). Here, we extend it with a complete characterization of the leading order fluctuation dynamics. For this purpose, we correct the latter via an appropriate Bogoliubov rotation, in partial analogy to the approach by Grillakis-Machedon et al. [ 60 ] , in addition to the Weyl transformation applied in [ 37 ] . Based on the analysis of the third order expansion of the BEC wave function, and the second order expansions of the pair-correlations, we show that through a renormalization strategy, various contributions to the effective Hamiltonian can be iteratively eliminated by an appropriate choice of the Weyl and Bogoliubov transformations. This leads to a separation of renormalized Hartree-Fock-Bogoliubov (HFB) equations and quantum Boltzmann equations. A multitude of terms that were included in the error term in [ 37 ] are now identified as contributions to the HFB renormalization terms. Thereby, the error bound in the work at hand is improved significantly. To the given order, it is now sharp, and matches the order or magnitude expected from scaling considerations. Consequently, we extend the time of validity to t ∼ ( log ⁡ N ) 2 t\sim(\log N)^{2} compared to t ∼ ( log ⁡ N / log ⁡ log ⁡ N ) 2 t\sim(\log N/\log\log N)^{2} before. We expect our approach to be extensible to smaller orders in 1 N \frac{1}{N} .



Statements and Declarations

The authors do not declare financial or non-financial interests that are directly or indirectly related to the work submitted for publication.

Analysis of PDE
. We thank Esteban Cárdenas and Jacky Chong for helpful discussions.

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Summary of previous results

The Boltzmann (transport) equation describes the time-dependent behavior of the phase space probability distribution
f
of fluids and gases. It takes the form

(1.1)

(\partial_{t}+p\cdot\nabla_{x}){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f_{t}}\,=\,Q[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f_{t}}]\,,

where, for a classical gas,

(1.2)

Q^{\mathrm{(cl)}}[f](p)\,=\,\int_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}\mathrm{d}\omega\ \int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\mathrm{d}p_{*}\ b(\omega,|p-p_{*}|)(f(p^{\prime})f(p_{*}^{\prime})-f(p)f(p_{*}))\Big|_{\begin{subarray}{c}p^{\prime}=p+[\omega\cdot(p_{*}-p)]\omega\\
p_{*}^{\prime}=p_{*}-[\omega\cdot(p_{*}-p)]\omega\end{subarray}}\,.

In order to derive this equation, Boltzmann imposed the
Stosszahlansatz
or
molecular chaos assumption
, which requires that
all marginals of the joint distribution of the Bose gas particles factorize into products of one-particle marginals
. We will refer to this condition as
propagation of
factorization
1
1

1

In the context of the classical Boltzmann equation, it is referred to as
propagation of chaos
. However, the notion for quantum particles differs from the classical concept, which is why we refrain from this nomenclature in the present context.
.

To this day, the rigorous derivation of the Boltzmann equation, and possible corrections, for all physically relevant regimes remains a widely open problem. However, in some special cases, and for sufficiently short times, there has been progress both from a heuristic and mathematically rigorous perspective. Hilbert
[
62
]
famously asked for a rigorous justification of Boltzmann’s equations; the derivation of physical laws from a set of mathematical axioms is referred to as
Hilbert’s sixth problem
. A fundamental question that emerges in this context is how irreversibility of the (mesoscopic) Boltzmann equation, for which the entropy functional is non-decreasing, arises from the microscopic reversible many-body dynamics, see, e.g.,
[
68
]
.

In the case of classical gases, starting from the classical Liouville equation for an interacting
N
-particle gas, Boltzmann’s idea to show the propagation of factorization in the derivation of Boltzmann’s equation has been made rigorous, albeit for times that allow for at most one collision. The first rigorous, though incomplete, results go back to Cercignani
[
33
]
and Lanford
[
67
]
, and were later completed
[
91
,
35
,
34
,
53
]
. A crucial insight in the derivation is that the joint distribution function only needs to factorize for particles that are
about to collide
. The derivation of the classical Boltzmann equation remains an extraordinarily active area of research, see, e.g.,
[
2
,
17
,
18
,
43
,
54
,
88
]
.

For a quantum gas or fluid, Nordheim
[
83
]
proposed a quantum analogue of (
1.1
), for which the collision operator
Q
is given by

(1.3)

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{\mathrm{(lit)}}_{4}}[f](p)\,=&\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{p}_{4}\ \delta(p_{1}+p_{2}-p_{3}-p_{4})\delta(E(p_{1})+E(p_{2})-E(p_{3})-E(p_{4}))\\
&|{\mathcal{M}}_{22}(\mathbf{p}_{4})|^{2}(\delta(p-p_{1})+\delta(p-p_{2})-\delta(p-p_{3})-\delta(p-p_{4}))\\
&\big((1\pm f(p_{1}))(1\pm f(p_{2}))f(p_{3})f(p_{4})-f(p_{1})f(p_{2})(1\pm f(p_{3}))(1\pm f(p_{4}))\big)\,,\end{split}

where ’+’ refers to the case of bosons, and ’-’ to fermions. Boldface letters with subscripts will denote multivectors, such as
\mathbf{p}_{k}=(p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{k})
, where
p_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
, and
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
{\mathcal{M}}_{22}(\mathbf{p}_{4})
is the scattering cross section relevant for this process. In the case of bosons, it has been shown
[
49
,
50
,
76
]
that the solution to the quantum Boltzmann equation with collision operator (
1.3
) develops a
\delta
-mass in finite time, corresponding to condensation in finite time, see also
[
3
,
4
,
1
]
for related works. If we then decompose
f=f^{(\mathrm{ex})}+n_{c}\delta
into its regular and singular part, we have that

(1.4)

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{\mathrm{(lit)}}_{4}}[f]\,=\,n_{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{\mathrm{(lit)}}_{3}}[f^{(\mathrm{ex})}]\,+\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{\mathrm{(lit)}}_{4}}[f^{(\mathrm{ex})}]\,-\,n_{c}\delta\int\mathrm{d}q\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{\mathrm{(lit)}}_{3}}[f^{(\mathrm{ex})}](q)\,,

where

(1.5)

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{\mathrm{(lit)}}_{3}}[f](p)\,=\,&\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{p}_{3}\ \delta(p_{1}+p_{2}-p_{3})\delta(E(p_{1})+E(p_{2})-E(p_{3}))\\
&|{\mathcal{M}}_{22}(\mathbf{p}_{3},0)|^{2}(\delta(p-p_{1})+\delta(p-p_{2})-\delta(p-p_{3}))\\
&\big((1+f(p_{1}))(1+f(p_{2}))f(p_{3})-f(p_{1})f(p_{2})(1+f(p_{3}))\big)\,.\end{split}

This leads to the coupled system

(1.6)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f^{(\mathrm{ex})}_{t}}\,&=\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n_{c}(t)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{\mathrm{(lit)}}_{3}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f^{(\mathrm{ex})}_{t}}]\,+\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{\mathrm{(lit)}}_{4}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f^{(\mathrm{ex})}_{t}}]\\
\partial_{t}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n_{c}(t)}\,&=\,-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n_{c}(t)}\int\mathrm{d}q\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{\mathrm{(lit)}}_{3}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f^{(\mathrm{ex})}_{t}}](q)\,.\end{cases}

If
n_{c}\gg 1
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{\mathrm{(lit)}}_{3}}[f^{(\mathrm{ex})}]
determines the leading order dynamics of
f^{(\mathrm{ex})}
. In this work, we are interested in studying the emergence of (
1.5
) for an interacting quantum Bose gas with initial condensate density

n_{c}(0)=N\gg 1
and initial thermal excitation density
f^{(\mathrm{ex})}_{0}\sim 1
.

In order to study the emergence of (
1.3
), we consider the Hamiltonian

(1.7)

H_{N,{\varepsilon}_{1},{\varepsilon}_{2}}\,:=\,\sum_{j=1}^{N}\frac{{\varepsilon}_{1}^{2}}{2}(-\Delta_{x_{j}})\,+\,g_{N,{\varepsilon}_{1},{\varepsilon}_{2}}\sum_{j<k}^{N}v\Big(\frac{x_{j}-x_{k}}{{\varepsilon}_{2}}\Big)

acting on the bosonic Hilbert space
L^{2}(\Lambda)^{\otimes_{s}N}
, where
\Lambda\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
is a 3-torus. Given the Schrödinger equation

(1.8)

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i{\varepsilon}_{1}\partial_{t}\Psi_{N,{\varepsilon}_{1},{\varepsilon}_{2},t}\,=\,H_{N,{\varepsilon}_{1},{\varepsilon}_{2}}\Psi_{N,{\varepsilon}_{1},{\varepsilon}_{2},t}\,,}

we are interested in the asymptotic behavior of the Wigner transforms

(1.9)

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f_{N,{\varepsilon}_{1},{\varepsilon}_{2},t}^{(k)}({\bf x}_{k},\mathbf{p}_{k}):=\int_{\Lambda^{N}}\mathrm{d}{\bf y}_{N}\ e^{i\mathbf{p}_{k}\cdot{\bf y}_{k}}\overline{\Psi}_{N,{\varepsilon}_{1},{\varepsilon}_{2},t}({\bf x}_{k}+\frac{{\varepsilon}_{1}}{2}{\bf y}_{k},{\bf y}_{N-k})\Psi_{N,{\varepsilon}_{1},{\varepsilon}_{2},t}({\bf x}_{k}-\frac{{\varepsilon}_{1}}{2}{\bf y}_{k},{\bf y}_{N-k}),}

where
{\bf y}_{N}=({\bf y}_{k},{\bf y}_{N-k})
. The propagation of chaos assumption in this case reads

(1.10)

f_{N,{\varepsilon}_{1},{\varepsilon}_{2},t}^{(k)}\,\approx\,\big(f_{N,{\varepsilon}_{1},{\varepsilon}_{2},t}^{(1)}\big)^{\otimes k}\,.

at positive times
t>0
for particles about to collide. The first mathematically rigorous results go back to Hugenholtz
[
64
]
and Ho-Landau
[
63
]
, where it was shown that the terms proportional to
g^{2}
in the Duhamel expansion of
f_{N,{\varepsilon}_{1},{\varepsilon}_{2},t}^{(1)}
give rise to a Boltzmann collision term, see also works by Benedetto et al.
[
10
,
11
]
. Under the assumption of
quantum propagation of factorization
, more precisely propagation of
restricted quasifreeness
, see Definition
2.1
below, Erdös-Salmhofer-Yau
[
45
]
showed that, for mesoscopic times
t\propto g^{-2}
, the second order Duhamel expansion of
f_{N,{\varepsilon}_{1},{\varepsilon}_{2},t}^{(1)}
yields the quantum Boltzmann equation (
1.3
). Lukkarinen and Spohn
[
77
]
later revisited this idea and stated conditions under which they derive a Boltzmann equation. However, they did not justify assumptions on the growth of certain moments for the evolution of
f^{(k)}_{N,t}
that would correspond to a rigorous error control in the evolution.

X. Chen and Guo
[
39
]
showed in the case
\Omega={\mathbb{R}}^{3}
that, if one assumes a sufficiently regular potential
v
, and if one assumes the convergence and sufficient regularity of the marginals
f_{N,{\varepsilon}_{1},{\varepsilon}_{2},t}^{(k)}
, then, in the mesoscopic weak-coupling regime, i.e.,
{\varepsilon}_{1}={\varepsilon}_{2}=N^{-1/3}
,
g_{N,{\varepsilon}_{1},{\varepsilon}_{2}}=\sqrt{{\varepsilon}_{1}}
, the limiting dynamics is given by a classical,
quadratic
Boltzmann equation (
1.2
).

In a recent work
[
41
]
, X. Chen and Holmer proved that, if the regularity
r
of marginals of the many-body joint distribution is propagated and its non-negativity are propagated, there exists a critical threshold
r_{c}
such that the following trichotomy holds: 1) If
r>r_{c}
, then the limiting dynamics of
f
is trivial; 2) if
r<r_{c}
, the effective Boltzmann equation is ill-posed; 3) if
r=r_{c}
, the limiting Boltzmann dynamics is
quadratic
, i.e., classical, see (
1.2
), instead of quantum, see (
1.3
). They study a factorization property that differs from that considered in the present work. Their factorization property, which is formulated in first quantization rather than second quantization, coincides with ours only in the asymptotic limit that the volume goes to infinity.

The existence of both
positive
and
negative

conditional
results suggests that a more detailed analysis of the fluctuation dynamics is required, in order to understand the validity of (cubic or quartic) quantum Boltzmann dynamics.

In the case of fermions, Cárdenas and one of the authors
[
31
]
established the emergence of quantum Boltzmann fluctuations alongside bosonized self-interaction terms, beyond the Hartree-Fock approximation, while rigorously controling the error. Their result is unconditional and holds for sufficiently short times. The bosonized self-interaction terms have been analyzed in more detail in
[
13
,
15
,
42
]
in the stationary case, and
[
12
]
for the dynamical case. Their results show that these can be characterized as
Random-Phase approximation
, as introduced by Bohm and Pines
[
19
,
86
,
20
]
.

The
mean-field regime
is determined by
{\varepsilon}_{1}={\varepsilon}_{2}=1
and
g=\frac{\lambda}{N}
, where
N
is the number of particles. For bosons, a question of special interest is the persistence of a Bose-Einstein condensate, i.e., the
N\gg 1
asymptotic behavior of the wave function
\Psi_{N,t}
satisfying (
1.8
) when, initially,
\Psi_{N,0}\approx\phi_{0}^{\otimes N}
. Going back to Ginibre-Velo
[
55
]
and Hepp
[
61
]
, it has been shown that the wave function remains factorized, i.e.,
\Psi_{N,t}\approx\phi_{t}^{\otimes N}
, where
\phi_{t}
satisfies a
nonlinear Hartree
(NLH) or
Schrödinger
(NLS) equation, dependent on the scaling regime. In particular, this establishes persistence of the condensate for microscopic times
t=O(1)
. These works have been extended, and convergence has been shown in different topologies
[
48
,
47
,
52
,
60
,
66
,
73
,
90
]
and for more singular scalings
{\varepsilon}_{2}=N^{-\beta}
,
g=N^{3\beta-1}
,
\beta\in[0,1]

[
27
,
38
,
36
,
40
,
44
,
46
,
58
,
59
,
85
]
. The case
\beta=1
is of particular physical interest, as it describes rare but strong interactions, and is referred to as the
Gross-Pitaevskii regime
. Another related problem is to study the asymptotic behavior of the ground state (energy) of (
1.7
), see, e.g.,
[
9
,
26
,
32
,
69
,
70
,
74
,
79
,
82
,
81
]
.

In order to study corrections to the leading order NLH/NLS BEC dynamics, it is necessary to include to study the dynamics of pair correlations, corresponding to thermal fluctuations. It has been shown
[
60
,
57
,
58
,
78
,
80
,
84
]
that these are governed by the nonlinear
Hartree-Fock-Bogoliubov
(HFB) equations. Bach, Breteaux, Fröhlich, Sigal and one of the authors
[
7
,
8
]
have shown that the
quasifree approximation
of the full dynamics, i.e., the restriction to states for which quantum propagation of factorization holds, is given by the HFB equations.

Another vibrant area of research is the (bosonic) ground state problem associated with (
1.7
), see
[
16
,
28
,
30
,
75
,
74
,
72
]
. There, corrections to the leading order Gross-Pitaevskii energy are described by the
Lee-Huang-Yang formula
, see, e.g.,
[
51
,
56
]
, and higher oder corrections have also been described
[
23
,
30
]
.

In our previous work
[
37
]
, we studied the regime
g=\frac{\lambda_{N}}{N}
for some
\lambda_{N}\ll 1
and
N\gg 1
, on a 3-torus
\Lambda
, for mesoscopic times
t\propto\lambda_{N}^{-2}
. The Bose gas initially consisted of a translation invariant BEC of
density

N
, and translation invariant thermal fluctuations close to equilibrium of density
1
, described by a
quasifree
state, see Definition
2.1
below.
In short, a quantum state is quasifree if its observable expectations satisfy a factorization property, that allows to reduce many-body expectations to single-particle expectations. The precise definition requires to properly introduce the mathematical framework needed to describe many-body dynamics, and we refer the reader to Section
2
.

Assuming
\hat{v}(0)=0
, we showed that, subleading to the HFB fluctuation dynamics, for kinetic times
t\propto\lambda^{-2}
, the density of thermal fluctuations obeys a (mollified) cubic Boltzmann equation with collision operator

(1.11)

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{\mathrm{(mol)}}_{3}}[f](p)\,=\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\frac{1}{N|\Lambda|}}&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{j}\in{\Lambda^{*}},\\
j=1,2,3\end{subarray}}\delta_{t}(\Omega(p_{1})+\Omega(p_{2})-\Omega(p_{3})){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta_{p_{1}+p_{2},p_{3}}|\hat{v}(p_{1})+\hat{v}(p_{2})|^{2}}\\
&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\times\big(\delta_{p,p_{1}}+\delta_{p,p_{2}}-\delta_{p,p_{3}}\big)}\big(\tilde{f}(p_{1})\tilde{f}(p_{2})f(p_{3})-f(p_{1})f(p_{2})\tilde{f}(p_{3})\big)\,,\end{split}

where
{\Lambda^{*}}
denotes the momentum (reciprocal) lattice
and
\tilde{f}=1+f
. These collisions correspond to those, where a BEC particle is either absorbed or emitted in the collision. Here
\delta_{t}
is a mollification of a
one-dimensional Dirac-
\delta
.

\Omega(p)=\sqrt{E(p)(E(p)+2\lambda\hat{v}(p))}
is the Bogoliubov dispersion, and we choose the BEC wavefunction to be constant equal to

|\Lambda|^{-\frac{1}{2}}
. Given a time scale shorter than
O(|\Lambda|^{\frac{1}{3}})
, where we assumed
\Lambda
to be a
cubic
torus in three dimensions, we showed that the discrete Boltzmann operator in (
1.11
) can be approximated by a continuous Boltzmann operator, where the summation

\frac{1}{|\Lambda|}\sum_{p\in{\Lambda^{*}}}
is replaced by Riemann integrals
(2\pi)^{3}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\mathrm{d}p\
,
\delta_{t}
by a Dirac-
\delta
and
|\Lambda|\delta_{p,0}
by a Dirac-
\delta

\delta(p)
. For longer times, interference leads to additional resonance terms. Crucially to our work, we used the fact that the HFB dynamics preserves quasifreeness, and showed that the full dynamics approximately preserves restricted quasifreeness for times
t\propto\lambda^{-2}\propto\big(\frac{\log N}{\log\log N}\big)^{\alpha}
,
\alpha>0
. Our result was a first unconditional result,
in the sense that propagation of quasifreeness for the considered time is proved
, and it provided a rigorous error control for sufficiently short times.

1.2.
New contributions

In the present work, we revisit this problem from a different perspective. We show that corrections to the pair-absorption rate and to the BEC wave function can be absorbed into the HFB equations. This, in turn, leads to a renormalization of the HFB equations describing the coupled dynamics of the BEC wave function with the leading order thermal pair-excitations. After renormalizing, the corrections to the BEC and thermal fluctuation dynamics are given by pure Boltzmann collision terms. More precisely, let
(\phi_{N,t},\gamma_{N,t},\sigma_{N,t})
denote the renormalized HFB fields, and
\Omega_{N,t}
be the corresponding renormalized Bogoliubov dispersion. Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f^{(\mathrm{tot})}_{N,t}}
denote the total density of the Bose gas,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Phi^{(\mathrm{tot})}_{N,t}}
denote the full BEC wavefunction, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}g^{(\mathrm{tot})}_{N,t}}
the full pair-absorption rate
, each evolving according to the many-body dynamics.
These fields can be expanded in the form

(1.12)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\Phi^{(\mathrm{tot})}_{N,t}\\
f^{(\mathrm{tot})}_{N,t}\,-\,|\Phi^{(\mathrm{tot})}_{N,t}|^{2}\delta\\
g^{(\mathrm{tot})}_{N,t}\,-\,(\Phi^{(\mathrm{tot})}_{N,t})^{2}\delta\end{pmatrix}\,=

\displaystyle\,\begin{pmatrix}\sqrt{N|\Lambda|}\phi_{N,t}\\
\gamma_{N,t}\\
\sigma_{N,t}\end{pmatrix}\,+\,\begin{pmatrix}a_{N,t}&0\\
0&A_{N,t}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\Phi_{N,t}\\
f_{N,t}-|\Phi_{N,t}|^{2}\delta\\
g_{N,t}-\Phi_{N,t}^{2}\delta\end{pmatrix}\,,

where

a_{N,t}\in{\mathbb{C}}
,
A_{N,t}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{2\times 2}
depend
on the HFB fields

(\phi_{N,t},\gamma_{N,t},\sigma_{N,t},\Omega_{N,t})
, and

\gamma_{N,t}
and

\sigma_{N,t}
are related by

|\sigma_{N,t}|^{2}=\gamma_{N,t}(1+\gamma_{N,t})
.

(
1.12
) can be interpreted as a gradual centering process:

(1)

We collect all condensate terms to given order in the perturbation expansion and add them to the contribution coming from

\phi_{N,t}
, and center the correlation functions

f_{N,t}
,
g_{N,t}
w.r.t.

\phi_{N,t}
. Then

\Phi_{N,t}
denotes the correction terms to given order.

(2)

Simultaneously, we subtract the leading order pair-correlations

(\gamma_{N,t},\sigma_{N,t})
from the centered pair correlations. Then

f_{N,t},g_{N,t}
denote the pair correlation corrections to given order.

We show that,
if

(\phi_{N,t},\gamma_{N,t},\sigma_{N,t},\Omega_{N,t})
satisfy the renormalized HFB equations

(1.13)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}i\partial_{t}\phi_{N,t}\,&=\,\frac{\lambda}{N}\big[\big(\Gamma_{N,t}*(\hat{v}+\hat{v}(0))\big)(0)\phi_{N,t}\,+\,(\Sigma_{N,t}*\hat{v})(0)\overline{\phi}_{N,t}\big]\,-\,2\lambda|\Lambda|\hat{v}(0)|\phi_{N,t}|^{2}\phi_{N,t}\,,\\
\partial_{t}\gamma_{N,t}\,&=\,\frac{2\lambda}{N}\operatorname{Im}\big((\Sigma_{N,t}*\hat{v})\overline{\sigma}_{N,t}\big)\,,\\
i\partial_{t}\sigma_{N,t}\,&=\,2\big(E+\frac{\lambda}{N}\Gamma_{N,t}*(\hat{v}+\hat{v}(0))\big)\sigma_{N,t}\,+\,\frac{\lambda}{N}\big(\Sigma_{N,t}*\hat{v}\big)(1+2\gamma_{N,t})\,,\\
\Omega_{N,t}\,&=\,E+\frac{\lambda}{N}\big(\Gamma_{N,t}*(\hat{v}+\hat{v}(0))\big)\,+\,\frac{\lambda}{N}\frac{\operatorname{Re}((\overline{\Sigma}_{N,t}*\hat{v})\sigma_{N,t})}{1+\gamma_{N,t}}\,,\end{cases}

where

(1.14)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{N,t}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma_{N,t}}\end{pmatrix}(p)\,=\,N|\Lambda|\delta(p)\begin{pmatrix}|{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi_{N,t}}|^{2}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi_{N,t}}^{2}\end{pmatrix}\,+\,\big((1+f_{0}(p)+f_{0}(-p))\begin{pmatrix}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma_{N,t}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sigma_{N,t}}\end{pmatrix}(p)\,+\,\begin{pmatrix}\frac{f_{0}(p)+f_{0}(-p)}{2}\\
0\end{pmatrix}\,,

and
E(p)=\frac{1}{2}|p|^{2}
,
then

(\Phi_{N,t},f_{N,t},g_{N,t})
satisfy

(1.15)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Phi_{N,t}}-\Phi_{0}\,

\displaystyle=\,\frac{1}{N^{\frac{3}{2}}}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\ \big(Q_{3}^{(\Phi)}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f_{N,\cdot}}](s)+Q_{3,3}^{(\Phi)}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f_{N,\cdot}}](s)\big)\,+\,O\Big(\frac{e^{c|\Lambda|\lambda t}}{N^{2}}\Big)\,,

(1.16)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f_{N,t}}-f_{0}\,

\displaystyle=\,\frac{1}{N}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\ Q_{3}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f_{N,\cdot}}](s)\,+\,O\Big(\frac{e^{c|\Lambda|\lambda t}}{N^{\frac{3}{2}}}\Big)\,,

(1.17)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}g_{N,t}}-g_{0}\,

\displaystyle=\,\frac{1}{N}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\ Q_{3}^{(g)}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f_{N,\cdot}}](s)\,+\,O\Big(\frac{e^{c|\Lambda|\lambda t}}{N^{\frac{3}{2}}}\Big)\,,

where each
Q_{j}^{(k)}
denotes a collection of cubic Boltzmann collision terms, for which the collision kernels depend on the renormalized HFB fields

(\phi_{N,t},\gamma_{N,t},\sigma_{N,t},\Omega_{N,t})
. Crucially, the errors provided here are
sharp
in orders of
N
.

In particular, we show that, to leading orders, the BEC wave function

\Phi^{(\mathrm{tot})}_{N,t}
and the pair correlations

(f^{(\mathrm{tot})}_{N,t},g^{(\mathrm{tot})}_{N,t})
can each be decomposed into a part that satisfies renormalized HFB equations and a quantum Boltzmann correction part. This separation confirms the phenomenological paradigm that the HFB and QBE dynamics evolve according to different time scales; the HFB dynamics leads to fast oscillations, while the QBE determines the slow long-time dynamics. As in our previous work, our result is unconditional and rigorous, however, we extend the validity to times of order
t\propto\lambda^{-2}\sim(\log N)^{2}
, in contrast to

t\propto\lambda^{-2}\sim(\log N/\log\log N)^{2}
before.

The term
N^{-3/2}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\ Q_{3}^{(\Phi)}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f_{N,\cdot}}](s)
in the evolution of the BEC wave function justifies the Boltzmann term in the evolution of the condensate density, see (
1.6
). However, the additional
N^{-3/2}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\ Q_{3,3}^{(\Phi)}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f_{N,\cdot}}](s)
has previously not been included, despite of being of the same order of magnitude as the cubic Boltzmann term, see also
[
87
,
89
,
92
]
.

Boßmann et al.
[
21
,
24
,
22
]
have computed a full expansion of the correction dynamics in terms of the coupling constant. In comparison, in our current and past work
[
37
]
, we have been able to characterize terms in the expansion as stemming either from a HFB contribution, or a quantum Boltzmann correction.

We are able to rigorously control the error for times
t\propto\lambda^{-2}\sim(\log N)^{2}
. A more detailed calculation shows that, at order
\frac{1}{N^{2}}
, terms emerge that are neither of HFB nor of quantum Boltzmann type.

2.
Statement of results

We now introduce the second-quantization formalism underlying the results of this paper.

Let
\Lambda=[-L/2,L/2]^{3}/\sim
denote a cubic torus of length
L
, and let
{\Lambda^{*}}=(\frac{2\pi}{L}{\mathbb{Z}})^{3}
denote its reciprocal space. Let

(2.1)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}\,:=\,{\mathbb{C}}\oplus\bigoplus_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}L^{2}(\Lambda)^{\otimes_{s}n}

denote the
bosonic Fock space
, endowed with the inner product

(2.2)

\displaystyle\langle\Phi,\Psi\rangle_{{\mathcal{F}}}\,=\,\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}\langle\Psi^{(n)},\Phi^{(n)}\rangle_{L^{2}(\Lambda^{n})}\,.

For
\psi\in L^{2}(\Lambda)
, let

(2.3)

\displaystyle(a(\psi)\Psi)^{(n-1)}({\bf x}_{n-1})\,=\,\sqrt{n}\int_{\Lambda}\mathrm{d}x\ \overline{\psi}(x)\Psi^{(n)}(x,{\bf x}_{n-1})

denote the annihilation operator, and

(2.4)

\displaystyle(a^{\dagger}(\psi)\Psi)^{(n+1)}\,

\displaystyle:=\,\sqrt{n+1}P_{L^{2}(\Lambda)^{\otimes_{s}(n+1)}}\psi\otimes\Psi^{(n)}

(2.5)

\displaystyle=\,\frac{\sqrt{n+1}}{(n+1)!}\sum_{\pi\in{\mathcal{S}}_{n+1}}\psi(x_{\pi(1)})\Psi^{(n)}(x_{\pi(2)},\ldots,x_{\pi(n+1)})\,,

the creation operator. In addition, we introduce the momentum-space annihilation/creation operators
a_{p}^{\#}:=a^{\#}(e^{ip\cdot})
. They satisfy the CCR

(2.6)

\displaystyle[a_{p},a_{q}]\,=\,[a^{\dagger}_{p},a^{\dagger}_{q}]\,=\,0\,,\quad[a_{p},a^{\dagger}_{q}]\,=\,|\Lambda|\delta_{p,q}\,=:\,\delta_{{\Lambda^{*}}}(p-q)\,.

In the following, we will omit the subscript
{\Lambda^{*}}
, when referring to a momentum-
\delta
. For brevity, we will use the notation

(2.7)

\int_{{\Lambda^{*}}}\mathrm{d}p\ h(p)\,:=\,\frac{1}{|\Lambda|}\sum_{p\in{\Lambda^{*}}}h(p)\,.

Again, we will omit the subscript
{\Lambda^{*}}
, unless it is ambiguous. Our convention for the Fourier transform is

(2.8)

\displaystyle\hat{h}(p)\,:=\,\int_{\Lambda}\mathrm{d}x\ e^{ip\cdot x}h(x)\,.

We consider the mean-field Hamiltonian

(2.9)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{N}\,:=\,\int\mathrm{d}p\ E(p)a^{\dagger}_{p}a_{p}+\frac{\lambda}{2N}\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{p}_{4}\ \delta(p_{1}+p_{2}-p_{3}-p_{4})\hat{v}(p_{1}-p_{3})\,a^{\dagger}_{p_{1}}a^{\dagger}_{p_{2}}a_{p_{3}}a_{p_{4}}\,,

where
E(p):=\frac{1}{2}|p|^{2}
denotes the free dispersion, and
v\geq 0
a pair potential such that
\hat{v}\geq 0
.

We are interested in determining the leading order dynamics for a Bose gas governed by the Hamiltonian
{\mathcal{H}}_{N}
. As an initial state, we will choose a Bose-Einstein condensate (BEC) of density
N
, surrounded by a thermal excitations that are described by a
quasifree
state with density
O(1)
.

2.1.
Initial state

Let

(2.10)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{W}}[f]\,:=\,\exp\big(a^{\dagger}(f)-a(f)\big)

denote the Weyl
operator
, and define
Bogoliubov operator

(2.11)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}[k]\,:=\,\exp\Big(\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}p\ \big(k(p)a^{\dagger}_{p}a^{\dagger}_{-p}-\overline{k}(p)a_{p}a_{-p}\big)\Big)

in the translation invariant case. Without loss of generality, we assume that
k
is even. We then obtain the Weyl transform

(2.12)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{W}}^{\dagger}[f]a_{p}{\mathcal{W}}[f]\,=\,a_{p}\,+\,\hat{f}(p)\,,

and the Bogoliubov transform

(2.13)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}^{\dagger}[k]a_{p}{\mathcal{T}}[k]\,=\,\cosh\big(|k(p)|\big)a_{p}\,+\,\sinh\big(|k(p)|\big)\frac{k(p)}{|k(p)|}a^{\dagger}_{-p}\,,

see Lemma
A.1
.
As a result, the creation and annihilation operators defined by (
2.12
) and (
2.13
) describe thermal fluctuations outside the BEC.

Let
{\mathfrak{A}}
denotes the CCR algebra generated by the Weyl operators
{\mathcal{W}}[\psi]
,
\psi\in L^{2}(\Lambda)
, see
[
25
, section 5.2.3]
.

Definition 2.1

(Quasifree state)
.

Let
\langle\cdot\rangle
be a state and

(2.14)

\displaystyle\langle A\rangle^{\mathrm{(cen)}}\,:=\,\langle({\mathcal{W}}[\langle a\rangle)]A{\mathcal{W}}^{\dagger}[\langle a\rangle])\rangle

denote its centering. We say
\langle\cdot\rangle
is
quasifree
iff for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}

(2.15)

\begin{cases}\langle a^{\#_{1}}a^{\#_{2}}\ldots a^{\#_{2n}}\rangle^{\mathrm{(cen)}}\,&=\,\hbox to5.29pt{\vbox to4.31pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.64294pt\lower-2.15277pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

) is referred to as
Wick’s Theorem
.

A state is
restricted quasifree
if (
2.15
) holds for

for some

.

Remark 2.2
.

\hbox to5.29pt{\vbox to4.31pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.64294pt\lower-2.15277pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.64294pt}{-2.15277pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{\#_{1}}\hbox to5.29pt{\vbox to4.31pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.64294pt\lower-2.15277pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{\#_{2}}\vbox to13.00002pt{}{}:=\langle a^{\#_{1}}a^{\#_{2}}\rangle^{\mathrm{(cen)}}
accounts for a
contraction
. The notation makes it easier to keep track of different terms in the Duhamel expansion.

Definition 2.3

(Number conserving state)
.

A state
\langle\cdot\rangle
is called
number conserving
iff
\langle[A,\mathcal{N}_{b}]\rangle=0
for every observable
A\in{\mathfrak{A}}
.

Definition 2.4

(Translation invariance)
.

A state
\langle\cdot\rangle
is called
translation invariant
iff

(2.16)

\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}a_{p_{j}}^{(\sigma_{j})}\rangle=\frac{\delta(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{j}p_{j})}{|\Lambda|}\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}a_{p_{j}}^{(\sigma_{j})}\rangle

for all
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
and
\sigma_{j}=\pm 1
.

Let
\langle\cdot\rangle_{0}
be a number conserving, quasifree, and translation invariant. More precisely, let

(2.17)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}\,:=\,\int_{{\Lambda^{*}}}\mathrm{d}p\ K(p)a^{\dagger}_{p}a_{p}

be such that
K(p)\geq\kappa_{0}
for some
\kappa_{0}>0
, and let

(2.18)

\displaystyle\langle A\rangle_{0}\,:=\,\frac{\operatorname{Tr}(e^{-{\mathcal{K}}}A)}{\operatorname{Tr}(e^{-{\mathcal{K}}})}\,.

Observe that, by being number conserving,
\langle\cdot\rangle_{0}
is already centered.
\langle\cdot\rangle_{0}
determines the thermal excitations beyond the HFB fluctuations.

The full state describing the Bose gas is then given by

(2.19)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\langle A\rangle^{(\mathrm{tot})}_{N,0}}\,:=\,\langle e^{-{\mathcal{K}}}{\mathcal{T}}^{\dagger}[k_{0}]{\mathcal{W}}^{\dagger}[\sqrt{N|\Lambda|}\phi_{0}]A{\mathcal{W}}[\sqrt{N|\Lambda|}\phi_{0}]{\mathcal{T}}[k_{0}]\rangle_{0}

for all
A\in{\mathfrak{A}}
. Note that

\langle\cdot\rangle^{(\mathrm{tot})}_{N,0}
is quasifree.
Our initial state is chosen close to a Gibbs state, see, e.g.,
[
71
, Theorem 2.3]
.
The initial value problem (IVP) associated with the Hamiltonian
{\mathcal{H}}_{N}
and the initial state
\langle\cdot\rangle^{(\mathrm{tot})}_{0}
is then given by the Liouville-von Neumann equation

(2.20)

\displaystyle i\partial_{t}\langle A\rangle^{(\mathrm{tot})}_{N,t}\,=\,\langle[A,{\mathcal{H}}_{N}]\rangle^{(\mathrm{tot})}_{N,t}

for all observables
A\in{\mathfrak{A}}
. Below, we impose assumptions on
v
ensuring that
{\mathcal{H}}_{N}
is self-adjoint and that it induces a unitary evolution
e^{-it{\mathcal{H}}_{N}}
. Notice that
\langle\cdot\rangle^{(\mathrm{tot})}_{t}
is not quasifree. However, we will show that for short enough times,
\langle\cdot\rangle^{(\mathrm{tot})}_{t}
is approximately quasifree. Consequently, the evolution of any expectation
\langle A\rangle^{(\mathrm{tot})}_{t}
is fully characterized by the first and second moments
\langle a_{0}\rangle^{(\mathrm{tot})}_{t}
, and
\langle a^{\dagger}_{p}a_{p}\rangle^{(\mathrm{tot})}_{t}
,
\langle a_{p}a_{-p}\rangle^{(\mathrm{tot})}_{t}
, respectively.

Of particular interest in the present work is the evolution of the density

(2.21)

\displaystyle f^{(\mathrm{tot})}_{N,t}(p)\,:=\,\frac{\langle a^{\dagger}_{p}a_{p}\rangle^{(\mathrm{tot})}_{N,t}}{|\Lambda|}\,.

In order to study

f^{(\mathrm{tot})}_{N,t}
, we will decompose the full dynamics into a BEC and a thermal fluctuation part. For the latter, we will further decompose the dynamics of the fluctuation particles into a quasifree part, which we will refer to as
Hartree-Fock-Bogoliubov fluctuations
, and a collision part, which we will identify as the
quantum Boltzmann fluctuations
.

Our approach includes the time-behavior of leading order pair correlations via Bogoliubov rotations as employed by Grillakis-Machedon et al.
[
60
,
57
,
58
]
to derive the leading order HFB dynamics. However, we extend the latter by a quasifree, number conserving, centered (translation invariant) state. It is the presence of these additional excitation states that allow for a (cubic) Boltzmann equation to arise.

Subsequently, we will suppress the dependence of states and fields on the BEC density
N
from the notations for
(\Phi^{(\mathrm{tot})},f^{(\mathrm{tot})},g^{(\mathrm{tot})})
and the relative fields
(\Phi,f,g)
.

2.2.
Fluctuation dynamics

In order to extract the evolution of thermal fluctuations surrounding the BEC, we pass to the relative evolution relative the BEC. To this end, we define the Bogoliubov propagation

(2.22)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}i\partial_{t}{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathrm{Bog}}(t)&=\int\mathrm{d}p\ \Omega_{t}(p)a_{p}^{\dagger}a_{p}{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathrm{Bog}}(t)\,,\\
{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathrm{Bog}}(0)&={\bf 1}\,,\end{cases}

where
\Omega_{t}
is an even function. We have that

(2.23)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathrm{Bog}}^{\dagger}(t)a_{p}{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathrm{Bog}}(t)\,=\,e^{-i\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\ \Omega_{s}(p)}a_{p}\,.

Recalling the Weyl transform and the Bogoliubov transform obtained from conjugation by
{\mathcal{W}}
and
{\mathcal{T}}
, respectively,
we introduce the
evolution operator for the

fluctuation dynamics
defined by

(2.24)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathrm{fluc}}(t)\,:=\,e^{iS_{t}}{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathrm{Bog}}^{\dagger}(t){\mathcal{T}}^{\dagger}[k_{t}]{\mathcal{W}}^{\dagger}[\sqrt{N|\Lambda|}\phi_{t}]e^{-it{\mathcal{H}}_{N}}{\mathcal{W}}[\sqrt{N|\Lambda|}\phi_{0}]{\mathcal{T}}[k_{0}]\,,

where
(\phi_{t},k_{t})
and
S_{t}
will be determined below. We will choose
\phi_{t}
and
k_{t}
in such a way that the leading order and next-to-leading order contributions of the full dynamics
e^{-i{\mathcal{H}}_{N}t}
are determined by
(\phi_{t},k_{t})
.

We assume that
\phi_{t}(x)\equiv\phi_{t}
is translation invariant. We show in Lemma
A.2
that the fluctuation dynamics satisfies

(2.25)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}i\partial_{t}{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathrm{fluc}}(t)&={\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{fluc}}(t){\mathcal{U}}_{\mathrm{fluc}}(t)\,,\\
{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathrm{fluc}}(0)&={\bf 1}\,,\end{cases}

where
, for an appropriate choice of
S_{t}
,
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{fluc}}(t)
takes the form

(2.26)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{fluc}}(t)\,=\,{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{BEC}}(t)\,+\,{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{HFB}}(t)\,+\,{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{cub}}(t)\,+\,{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{quart}}(t)\,.

Each of the terms in
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{fluc}}(t)
is a normal-ordered polynomial in
a
and
a^{\dagger}
, and their explicit expressions are given in Lemma
A.2
. Here a monomial in
a
and
a^{\dagger}
is
normal-ordered
iff all creation operators
a^{\dagger}
are on the left of all annihilation operators
a
. We choose
S_{t}
in such a way that it absorbs all scalar terms.
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{BEC}}(t)
denotes the BEC Hamiltonian and it is linear in
a^{\#}
,
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{HFB}}
is the HFB Hamiltonian, which is quadratic in
a^{\#}
,
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{cub}}(t)
is cubic in
a^{\#}
and accounts for cubic scattering processes, where one of the particles is being absorbed into or emitted from the BEC, and
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{quart}}(t)
is quartic in
a^{\#}
and describes pair interactions.

Let

(2.27)

\displaystyle u_{t}(p):=

\displaystyle\cosh(|k_{t}(p)|)\,,

(2.28)

\displaystyle v_{t}(p):=

\displaystyle\sinh(|k_{t}(p)|)\frac{k_{t}(p)}{|k_{t}(p)|}\,.

In particular, we can rewrite (
2.13
) as

(2.29)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}^{\dagger}[k_{t}]a_{p}{\mathcal{T}}[k_{t}]\,=\,u_{t}(p)a_{p}\,+\,v_{t}(p)a^{\dagger}_{-p}\,.

Note that we have
u_{t}(p)^{2}-|v_{t}(p)|^{2}=1
. In addition, we introduce the scalar fields

(2.30)

\displaystyle\gamma_{t}(p):=

\displaystyle|v_{t}(p)|^{2}\,,

(2.31)

\displaystyle\sigma_{t}(p):=

\displaystyle u_{t}(p)v_{t}(p)\,.

Observe that, from this definition, we have the relation

(2.32)

\displaystyle|\sigma_{t}(p)|^{2}\,=\,u_{t}(p)^{2}|v_{t}(p)|^{2}\,=\,(1+\gamma_{t}(p))\gamma_{t}(p)\,.

Then the expressions for each of the normal-ordered terms in
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{fluc}}(t)
in (
2.26
) are given in Lemma
A.2
in the Appendix.

We introduce the time-evolved
relative
state

(2.33)

\langle A\rangle_{t}\,:=\,\langle{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathrm{fluc}}^{\dagger}(t)A{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathrm{fluc}}(t)\rangle_{0}\,.

It satisfies

(2.34)

i\partial_{t}\langle A\rangle_{t}\,=\,\langle[A,{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{fluc}}(t)]\rangle_{t}\,.

We introduce the relative moments

(2.35)

\displaystyle\Phi_{t}\,:=\,\frac{\langle a_{0}\rangle_{t}}{|\Lambda|}\,,\quad f_{t}(p)\,:=\,\frac{\langle a^{\dagger}_{p}a_{p}\rangle_{t}}{|\Lambda|}\,,\quad g_{t}(p)\,:=\,\frac{\langle a_{p}a_{-p}\rangle_{t}}{|\Lambda|}\,.

Our choice of
\langle\cdot\rangle_{0}
implies
\Phi_{0}=g_{0}=0
. Then we can rewrite the total density
f^{(\mathrm{tot})}
, see (
2.21
) for its definition and section (
3.1
) for the derivation, as

(2.36)

\displaystyle f^{(\mathrm{tot})}_{t}(p)\,=

\displaystyle\,\delta(p)\Big[N|\Lambda||\phi_{t}|^{2}\,+\,\Big(\sqrt{N|\Lambda|}e^{i\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\ \Omega_{s}(0)}\big(\phi_{t}u_{t}(0)+\overline{\phi}_{t}v_{t}(0)\big)\overline{\Phi}_{t}\,+\,\mathrm{h.c.}\Big)\Big]

(2.37)

\displaystyle\,+\,\gamma_{t}(p)\,+\,\big(1+\gamma_{t}(p)\big)f_{t}(p)\,+\,\gamma_{t}(p)f_{t}(-p)\,+\,\Big(e^{2i\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\ \Omega_{s}(p)}\sigma_{t}(p)\overline{g}_{t}(p)+\mathrm{h.c.}\Big)\,.

Our goal is to show that if
(\phi_{t},\gamma_{t},\sigma_{t},\Omega_{t})
satisfy the
renormalized
Hartree-Fock-Bogoliubov (HFB) equations, then the dynamics of
(\Phi_{t},f_{t},g_{t})
each are determined, to leading order, by a Boltzmann equation. More precisely, we will show that, to leading order, the dynamics of
f
is given by a cubic Boltzmann equation, while the dynamics of
\Phi
and
g
, to leading order, are driven by
f
via a collision term, see (
1.15
)–(
1.17
).

2.3.
Main result

As a final step, we establish well-posedness of the leading order HFB dynamics, describing the BEC and the leading order thermal fluctuations. This is required for the statement of our main result.

2.3.1.
Renormalized HFB equations

Let

(2.38)

\displaystyle f_{0}^{(+)}(p)\,:=\,\frac{1}{2}\big(f_{0}(p)+f_{0}(-p)\big)

denote the even symmetrization of
f_{0}
. We introduce the (second order) renormalized HFB fields

(2.39)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{(2)}:=

\displaystyle(1+2f_{0}^{(+)})\gamma\,+\,f_{0}^{(+)}\,+\,N|\Lambda||\phi|^{2}\delta\,,

(2.40)

\displaystyle\Sigma^{(2)}:=

\displaystyle(1+2f_{0}^{(+)})\sigma\,+\,N|\Lambda|\phi^{2}\delta\,.

Then the renormalized HFB equations read

(2.41)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}i\partial_{t}\phi^{(2)}_{t}\,&=\,\frac{\lambda}{N}\Big(\big(\Gamma^{(2)}_{t}*(\hat{v}+\hat{v}(0))\big)(0)\phi^{(2)}_{t}\,+\,(\Sigma^{(2)}_{t}*\hat{v})(0)\overline{\phi}^{(2)}_{t}\Big)\\
&\qquad-\,2\lambda|\Lambda|\hat{v}(0)|\phi^{(2)}_{t}|^{2}\phi^{(2)}_{t}\,,\\
\partial_{t}\gamma^{(2)}_{t}\,&=\,\frac{2\lambda}{N}\operatorname{Im}\big((\Sigma^{(2)}_{t}*\hat{v})\overline{\sigma}^{(2)}_{t}\big)\,,\\
i\partial_{t}\sigma^{(2)}_{t}\,&=\,2\big(E+\frac{\lambda}{N}\Gamma^{(2)}_{t}*(\hat{v}+\hat{v}(0))\big)\sigma^{(2)}_{t}\,+\,\frac{\lambda}{N}\big(\Sigma^{(2)}_{t}*\hat{v}\big)(1+2\gamma^{(2)}_{t})\,,\end{cases}

and the corresponding Bogoliubov dispersion is given by

(2.42)

\displaystyle\Omega^{(2)}_{t}\,:=\,E+\frac{\lambda}{N}\big(\Gamma^{(2)}_{t}*(\hat{v}+\hat{v}(0))\big)\,+\,\frac{\lambda}{N}\frac{\operatorname{Re}\big(\overline{\Sigma}^{(2)}*\hat{v})\sigma^{(2)}_{t}\big)}{1+\gamma^{(2)}_{t}}\,.

Remark 2.5
.

Observe that
\Omega^{(2)}_{t}
is a
modified
Bogoliubov dispersion. Instead, the
regular
Bogoliubov dispersion, to leading order, is given by
\Omega_{\mathrm{Bog}}=\sqrt{E(E+2\lambda\hat{v})}
, where
E(p)=|p|^{2}/2
is the free dispersion, see above and, e.g.,
[
37
,
84
]
.

Omitting superscripts, we note that the HFB equations (
2.41
) can be rewritten as

(2.43)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}i\partial_{t}\phi_{t}\,&=\,\frac{\lambda}{N}\Big(\big(\Gamma_{t}*(\hat{v}+\hat{v}(0))\big)(0)\phi_{t}+(\Sigma_{t}*\hat{v})(0)\overline{\phi}_{t}\Big)-2\lambda|\Lambda|\hat{v}(0)|\phi_{t}|^{2}\phi_{t}\,,\\
\partial_{t}\Gamma_{t}\,&=\,\frac{2\lambda}{N}\operatorname{Im}\big((\Sigma_{t}*\hat{v})\overline{\Sigma}_{t}\big)\,,\\
i\partial_{t}\Sigma_{t}\,&=\,2\big(E+\frac{\lambda}{N}\Gamma_{t}*(\hat{v}+\hat{v}(0))\big)\Sigma_{t}\,+\,\frac{\lambda}{N}\big(\Sigma_{t}*\hat{v}\big)(1+2\Gamma_{t})\\
&\quad\quad-\,4N\lambda|\Lambda|^{2}\hat{v}(0)|\phi_{t}|^{2}\phi_{t}^{2}\delta\,.\end{cases}

For any
1\leq a<\infty
, we introduce the rescaled
L^{a}({\Lambda^{*}})
-norms

(2.44)

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L^{a}({\Lambda^{*}})}\,:=\,\|f\|_{a}\,:=\,|\Lambda|^{-\frac{1}{a}}\|f\|_{\ell^{a}({\Lambda^{*}})},\quad\|f\|_{L^{\infty}({\Lambda^{*}})}\,:=\,\|f\|_{\infty}:=\|f\|_{\ell^{\infty}({\Lambda^{*}})}\,.

For any weight
\widetilde{w}:{\Lambda^{*}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
, define the weighted
L^{r}
space

(2.45)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L^{r}_{\widetilde{w}}({\Lambda^{*}})\,:=\,\{f:{\Lambda^{*}}\to{\mathbb{R}}\mid\|\widetilde{w}^{\frac{1}{r}}f\|_{L^{r}({\Lambda^{*}})}<\infty\}\,,}

endowed with the norm

(2.46)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\|f\|_{L^{r}_{\widetilde{w}}}\,:=\,\|\widetilde{w}^{\frac{1}{r}}f\|_{r}\,.}

Based on the norms (
2.44
), we abbreviate

(2.47)

\displaystyle\|f\|_{X}\,

\displaystyle:=\,\|f\|_{1}\,+\,\|f\|_{\infty}\,,

(2.48)

\displaystyle\|f\|_{Y}\,

\displaystyle:=\,\|f\|_{L^{1}_{\sqrt{1+E}}}\,+\,\|f\|_{\infty}\,.

For all
j\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
, we introduce the function spaces

(2.49)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{X}}^{j}:=

\displaystyle{\mathbb{C}}\times\big(L^{1}_{(1+E)^{j}}({\Lambda^{*}})\cap L^{\infty}({\Lambda^{*}})\big)\times\big(L^{2}_{(1+E)^{j}}({\Lambda^{*}})\cap L^{\infty}({\Lambda^{*}})\big)\,,

endowed with the norm

(2.50)

\displaystyle\|(\phi,\Gamma,\Sigma)\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{j}}\,:=\,|\phi|\,+\,\|\Gamma\|_{L^{1}_{(1+E)^{j}}}\,+\,\|\Gamma\|_{\infty}\,+\,\|\Sigma\|_{L^{2}_{(1+E)^{j}}}\,+\,\|\Sigma\|_{\infty}\,.

In addition, we define

(2.51)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{X}}^{-j}\,:=\,({\mathcal{X}}^{j})^{\prime}\,,

in the sense of Banach space duals.

Definition 2.6

(Mild solution)
.

We call
(\phi,\Gamma,\Sigma)
a
mild solution
of (
2.43
) with initial datum
(\phi_{0},\Gamma_{0},\Sigma_{0})\in{\mathcal{X}}^{1}
iff there exists
T>0
such that
(\phi,\Gamma,\Sigma)\in C^{0}_{t}\big([0,T),{\mathcal{X}}^{1}\big)\cap C^{1}_{t}\big([0,T),{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}\big)
satisfies

(2.52)

\displaystyle\phi_{t}\,=\,

\displaystyle\phi_{0}\,-\,i\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\ \Big[\frac{\lambda}{N}\Big(\big(\Gamma_{s}*(\hat{v}+\hat{v}(0))\big)(0)\phi_{s}\,+\,(\Sigma_{s}*\hat{v})(0)\overline{\phi}_{s}\Big)

(2.53)

\displaystyle-\,2\lambda|\Lambda|\hat{v}(0)|\phi_{s}|^{2}\phi_{s}\Big]\,,

(2.54)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{t}\,=\,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{0}\,-\,\frac{2\lambda}{N}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\ \operatorname{Im}\Big(\big(\overline{\Sigma}_{s}*\hat{v}\big)\,\Sigma_{s}\Big)\,,

(2.55)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{t}\,=\,

\displaystyle e^{-2iEt}\Sigma_{0}\,-\,i\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\ e^{-2iE(t-s)}\Big[2\big(\frac{\lambda}{N}\Gamma_{s}*(\hat{v}+\hat{v}(0))\big)\Sigma_{s}

(2.56)

\displaystyle+\,\frac{\lambda}{N}\big(\Sigma_{s}*\hat{v}\big)(1+2\Gamma_{s})\,-\,4N\lambda|\Lambda|^{2}\hat{v}(0)|\phi_{s}|^{2}\phi_{s}^{2}\,\delta\Big]

for all
t\in[0,T)
.

For the next result, we introduce the truncated fields

(2.57)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{T}\,:=\,\Gamma-N|\Lambda||\phi|^{2}\delta\,,\quad\Sigma^{T}\,:=\,\Sigma-N|\Lambda|\phi^{2}\delta\,.

Proposition 2.7

(Global well-posedness)
.

Assume that
\hat{v}\in L^{1}_{\sqrt{1+E}}\cap L^{\infty}({\Lambda^{*}})
, and that
v\geq 0
. Let
(\phi_{0},\Gamma_{0},\Sigma_{0})\in{\mathcal{X}}^{1}
, such that
\Gamma^{T}_{0}\geq 0
and
|\Sigma^{T}_{0}|^{2}\leq(\Gamma^{T}_{0}+1)\Gamma^{T}_{0}
. Let
(\phi,\Gamma,\Sigma)\in C^{0}_{t}\big([0,T_{0}),{\mathcal{X}}^{1}\big)\cap C^{1}_{t}\big([0,T_{0}),{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}\big)
be the associated unique maximal mild solution of (
2.43
) with existence time
T_{0}>0
. Then
T_{0}=\infty
, and
\Gamma^{T}_{t}\geq 0
and
|\Sigma^{T}_{t}|^{2}\leq(\Gamma^{T}_{t}+1)\Gamma^{T}_{t}
for all
t\geq 0
.

2.3.2.
Boltzmann collision kernels

Our main result, Theorem
2.8
, depends on quantum Boltzmann collision kernels, which we introduce here. These are expressed in terms of the HFB fields
(\phi,\gamma,\sigma,\Omega)
, controlled by Proposition
2.7
.

In Lemma
A.2
, we compute the cubic collision kernels

(2.58)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathbf{w}^{(3,0)}_{t}(\mathbf{p}_{3})\,:=\\
&\sqrt{|\Lambda|}\Big(\big(u_{t}(p_{1})u_{t}(p_{2})v_{t}(p_{3})\phi_{t}+v_{t}(p_{1})v_{t}(p_{2})u_{t}(p_{3})\overline{\phi}_{t}\big)\big(\hat{v}(p_{1})+\hat{v}(p_{2})\big)\\
&+\,\big(v_{t}(p_{1})u_{t}(p_{2})u_{t}(p_{3})\phi_{t}+u_{t}(p_{1})v_{t}(p_{2})v_{t}(p_{3})\overline{\phi}_{t}\big)\big(\hat{v}(p_{2})+\hat{v}(p_{3})\big)\\
&+\,\big(u_{t}(p_{1})v_{t}(p_{2})u_{t}(p_{3})\phi_{t}+v_{t}(p_{1})u_{t}(p_{2})v_{t}(p_{3})\overline{\phi}_{t}\big)\big(\hat{v}(p_{1})+\hat{v}(p_{3})\big)\Big)\,,\end{split}

(2.59)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathbf{w}^{(2,1)}_{t}(\mathbf{p}_{3})\,:=\\
&\sqrt{|\Lambda|}\Big(\big(u_{t}(p_{1})u_{t}(p_{2})u_{t}(p_{3})\phi_{t}+v_{t}(p_{1})v_{t}(p_{2})\overline{v_{t}}(p_{3})\overline{\phi}_{t}\big)\big(\hat{v}(p_{1})+\hat{v}(p_{2})\big)\\
&+\big(v_{t}(p_{1})u_{t}(p_{2})\overline{v_{t}}(p_{3})\phi_{t}+u_{t}(p_{1})v_{t}(p_{2})u_{t}(p_{3})\overline{\phi}_{t}\big)\big(\hat{v}(p_{2})+\hat{v}(p_{3})\big)\\
&+\big(u_{t}(p_{1})v_{t}(p_{2})\overline{v_{t}}(p_{3})\phi_{t}+v_{t}(p_{1})u_{t}(p_{2})u_{t}(p_{3})\overline{\phi}_{t}\big)\big(\hat{v}(p_{1})+\hat{v}(p_{3})\big)\Big)\end{split}

in the expression for
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{cub}}(t)
. Analogously, we also obtain the quartic collision kernels

(2.60)

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}^{(4,0)}_{t}(\mathbf{p}_{4})\,:=

(2.61)

\displaystyle\big(u_{t}(p_{1})u_{t}(p_{2})v_{t}(p_{3})v_{t}(p_{4})+v_{t}(p_{1})v_{t}(p_{2})u_{t}(p_{3})u_{t}(p_{4})\big)\big(\hat{v}(p_{1}+p_{3})+\hat{v}(p_{2}+p_{3})\big)

(2.62)

\displaystyle+\,\big(u_{t}(p_{1})v_{t}(p_{2})u_{t}(p_{3})v_{t}(p_{4})+v_{t}(p_{1})u_{t}(p_{2})v_{t}(p_{3})u_{t}(p_{4})\big)\big(\hat{v}(p_{1}+p_{2})+\hat{v}(p_{2}+p_{3})\big)

(2.63)

\displaystyle+\,\big(u_{t}(p_{1})v_{t}(p_{2})v_{t}(p_{3})u_{t}(p_{4})+v_{t}(p_{1})u_{t}(p_{2})u_{t}(p_{3})v_{t}(p_{4})\big)\big(\hat{v}(p_{1}+p_{2})+\hat{v}(p_{1}+p_{3})\big)\,,

(2.64)

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}^{(3,1)}_{t}(\mathbf{p}_{4})\,:=

(2.65)

\displaystyle\big(u_{t}(p_{1})u_{t}(p_{2})v_{t}(p_{3})u_{t}(p_{4})+v_{t}(p_{1})v_{t}(p_{2})u_{t}(p_{3})\overline{v_{t}}(p_{4})\big)\big(\hat{v}(p_{1}+p_{3})+\hat{v}(p_{2}+p_{3})\big)

(2.66)

\displaystyle+\big(u_{t}(p_{1})v_{t}(p_{2})u_{t}(p_{3})u_{t}(p_{4})+v_{t}(p_{1})u_{t}(p_{2})v_{t}(p_{3})\overline{v_{t}}(p_{4})\big)\big(\hat{v}(p_{1}+p_{2})+\hat{v}(p_{2}+p_{3})\big)

(2.67)

\displaystyle+\,\big(v_{t}(p_{1})u_{t}(p_{2})u_{t}(p_{3})u_{t}(p_{4})+u_{t}(p_{1})v_{t}(p_{2})v_{t}(p_{3})\overline{v_{t}}(p_{4})\big)\big(\hat{v}(p_{1}+p_{2})+\hat{v}(p_{1}+p_{3})\big)\,,

(2.68)

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}^{(2,2)}_{t}(\mathbf{p}_{4})\,:=

(2.69)

\displaystyle\big(u_{t}(p_{1})u_{t}(p_{2})u_{t}(p_{3})u_{t}(p_{4})\,+\,v_{t}(p_{1})v_{t}(p_{2})\overline{v_{t}}(p_{3})\overline{v_{t}}(p_{3})\big)\big(\hat{v}(p_{1}-p_{3})+\hat{v}(p_{2}-p_{3})\big)

(2.70)

\displaystyle+\,\big(u_{t}(p_{1})v_{t}(p_{2})\overline{v_{t}}(p_{3})u_{t}(p_{4})+v_{t}(p_{1})u_{t}(p_{2})u_{t}(p_{3})\overline{v_{t}}(p_{4})\big)\big(\hat{v}(p_{1}+p_{2})+\hat{v}(p_{2}-p_{3})\big)

(2.71)

\displaystyle+\,\big(v_{t}(p_{1})u_{t}(p_{2})\overline{v_{t}}(p_{3})u_{t}(p_{4})+u_{t}(p_{1})v_{t}(p_{2})u_{t}(p_{3})\overline{v_{t}}(p_{4})\big)\big(\hat{v}(p_{1}+p_{2})+\hat{v}(p_{1}-p_{3})\big)\,.

in the expression for
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{quart}}(t)
. The Bogoliubov coefficients
u
and
v
are related to the HFB fields via

(2.72)

u_{t}(p)=\sqrt{1+\gamma_{t}(p)}\,,\quad v_{t}(p)=\frac{\sigma_{t}(p)}{\sqrt{1+\gamma_{t}(p)}}\,.

Moreover, we abbreviate

(2.73)

\displaystyle\widetilde{h}(p)\,

\displaystyle:=\,h(p)+1\,,

(2.74)

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbf{p}}\,

\displaystyle:=\,(p_{1},p_{2},-p_{3})\,,

(2.75)

\displaystyle\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\mathbf{p}$}}$}_{3}\,

\displaystyle:=\,(p_{3},p_{2},p_{1})\,.

Then we introduce the cubic Boltzmann operators

(2.76)

\displaystyle Q_{3}[h](t,p)\,:=

(2.77)

\displaystyle 2\lambda^{2}\operatorname{Re}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\ \int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{p}_{3}\ \Big(\frac{1}{2!}\big(\delta(p_{1}-p)+\delta(p_{2}-p)-\delta(p_{3}-p)\big)

(2.78)

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}^{(2,1)}_{s_{1}}(\mathbf{p}_{3})\overline{\mathbf{w}}^{(2,1)}_{s}(\mathbf{p}_{3})e^{i\int_{s}^{t}\mathrm{d}\tau\ \big(\Omega_{\tau}(p_{1})+\Omega_{\tau}(p_{2})-\Omega_{\tau}(p_{3})\big)}\delta(p_{1}+p_{2}-p_{3})

(2.79)

\displaystyle\big(\widetilde{h}_{s}(p_{1})\widetilde{h}_{s}(p_{2})h_{s}(p_{3})-h_{s}(p_{1})h_{s}(p_{2})\widetilde{h}_{s}(p_{3})\big)

(2.80)

\displaystyle+\,\frac{1}{3!}\big(\delta(p_{1}-p)+\delta(p_{2}-p)+\delta(p_{3}-p)\big)

(2.81)

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}^{(3,0)}_{s}(\mathbf{p}_{3})\overline{\mathbf{w}}^{(3,0)}_{s}(\mathbf{p}_{3})e^{i\int_{s}^{t}\mathrm{d}\tau\ \big(\Omega_{\tau}(p_{1})+\Omega_{\tau}(p_{2})+\Omega_{\tau}(p_{3})\big)}\delta(p_{1}+p_{2}+p_{3})

(2.82)

\displaystyle\big(\widetilde{h}_{s}(p_{1})\widetilde{h}_{s}(p_{2})\widetilde{h}_{s}(p_{3})-h_{s}(p_{1})h_{s}(p_{2})h_{s}(p_{3})\big)\Big)

and

(2.83)

\displaystyle Q_{3}^{(g)}[h](t)[J]\,:=

(2.84)

\displaystyle\lambda^{2}\int\mathrm{d}p\ J(p)\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\ \int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{p}_{3}\ \Big[\delta(p_{1}+p_{2}-p_{3})\Big(\delta(p-p_{3})e^{2i\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}\tau\ \Omega_{\tau}(p_{3})}

(2.85)

\displaystyle\quad e^{i\int_{s}^{s}\mathrm{d}\tau\ (\Omega_{\tau}(p_{1})+\Omega_{\tau}(p_{2})-\Omega_{\tau}(p_{3}))}\mathbf{w}^{(3,0)}_{t}(\overline{\mathbf{p}}_{3})\overline{\mathbf{w}}^{(2,1)}_{s_{2}}(\mathbf{p}_{3})-2\delta(p-p_{1})e^{-2i\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}\tau\ \Omega_{\tau}(p_{1})}

(2.86)

\displaystyle\quad e^{-i\int_{s}^{t}\mathrm{d}\tau\ (\Omega_{\tau}(p_{1})+\Omega_{\tau}(p_{2})-\Omega_{\tau}(p_{3}))}\overline{\mathbf{w}}^{(2,1)}_{t}(\overline{\mathbf{p}}_{3})\mathbf{w}^{(2,1)}_{s}(\mathbf{p}_{3})\Big)

(2.87)

\displaystyle\quad\big(h_{s}(p_{1})h_{s}(p_{2})\widetilde{h}_{s}(p_{3})-\widetilde{h}_{s}(p_{1})\widetilde{h}_{s}(p_{2})h_{s}(p_{3})\big)

(2.88)

\displaystyle+\,\delta(p-p_{3})e^{2i\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}\tau\ \Omega_{\tau}(p_{3})}\delta(p_{1}+p_{2}+p_{3})e^{-i\int_{s}^{t}\mathrm{d}\tau\ (\Omega_{\tau}(p_{1})+\Omega_{\tau}(p_{2})+\Omega_{\tau}(p_{3}))}

(2.89)

\displaystyle\quad\overline{\mathbf{w}}^{(2,1)}_{t}(\overline{\mathbf{p}}_{3})\mathbf{w}^{(3,0)}_{s}(\mathbf{p}_{3})\big(\widetilde{h}_{s}(p_{1})\widetilde{h}_{s}(p_{2})\widetilde{h}_{s}(p_{3})-h_{s}(p_{1})h_{s}(p_{2})h_{s}(p_{3})\big)\Big]

and

(2.90)

\displaystyle Q_{3}^{(\Phi)}[h](t)\,:=

(2.91)

\displaystyle\lambda^{2}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\ e^{i\int_{0}^{s_{1}}\mathrm{d}\tau\ \Omega_{\tau}(0)}\Big[\frac{1}{2}\delta(p_{1}+p_{2}-p_{3})\Big(e^{i\int_{s}^{t}\mathrm{d}\tau\ (\Omega_{\tau}(p_{1})+\Omega_{\tau}(p_{2})-\Omega_{\tau}(p_{3}))}

(2.92)

\displaystyle\quad\mathbf{w}^{(3,1)}_{t}(0,\mathbf{p}_{3})\overline{\mathbf{w}}^{(2,1)}_{s}(\mathbf{p}_{3})\,-\,e^{-i\int_{s}^{t}\mathrm{d}\tau\ (\Omega_{\tau}(p_{1})+\Omega_{\tau}(p_{2})-\Omega_{\tau}(p_{3}))}\mathbf{w}^{(2,2)}_{t}(0,\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\mathbf{p}$}}$}_{3})\mathbf{w}^{(2,1)}_{s}(\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\mathbf{p}$}}$}_{3})\Big)

(2.93)

\displaystyle\qquad\big(h_{s}(p_{1})h_{s}(p_{2})\widetilde{h}_{s}(p_{3})-\widetilde{h}_{s}(p_{1})\widetilde{h}_{s}(p_{2})h_{s}(p_{3})\big)

(2.94)

\displaystyle\quad+\,\frac{1}{3!}\delta(p_{1}+p_{2}+p_{3})\Big(\mathbf{w}^{(4,0)}_{t}(0,\mathbf{p}_{3})\overline{\mathbf{w}}^{(3,0)}_{s}(\mathbf{p}_{3})e^{i\int_{s}^{t}\mathrm{d}\tau\ (\Omega_{\tau}(p_{1})+\Omega_{\tau}(p_{2})+\Omega_{\tau}(p_{3}))}

(2.95)

\displaystyle\quad-\,\overline{\mathbf{w}}^{(3,1)}_{s_{1}}(\mathbf{p}_{3},0)\mathbf{w}^{(3,0)}_{s}(\mathbf{p}_{3})e^{-i\int_{s}^{t}\mathrm{d}\tau\ (\Omega_{\tau}(p_{1})+\Omega_{\tau}(p_{2})+\Omega_{\tau}(p_{3}))}\Big)

(2.96)

\displaystyle\qquad\big(h_{s}(p_{1})h_{s}(p_{2})h_{s}(p_{3})-\widetilde{h}_{s}(p_{1})\widetilde{h}_{s}(p_{2})\widetilde{h}_{s}(p_{3})\big)\Big]\,.

In the evolution of
\Phi
, we obtain the additional collision term

(2.97)

\displaystyle Q_{3,3}^{(\Phi)}[h](t)\,

\displaystyle:=\,\lambda e^{i\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}\tau\ \Omega_{\tau}(0)}\int\mathrm{d}p\ \Big(\mathbf{w}^{(2,1)}_{t}(0,p,p)Q_{3}[h](t,p)

(2.98)

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\,\overline{\mathbf{w}}^{(2,1)}_{t}(p,p,0)Q_{3}^{(g)}[h](t,p)\,+\,\mathbf{w}^{(3,0)}_{t}(p,p,0)\overline{Q_{3}^{(g)}[h](t,p)}\Big)\,.

2.3.3.
Main theorem

Theorem 2.8
.

Impose the same assumptions as in Proposition
2.7
, and let
\Omega^{(2)}
be the Bogoliubov dispersion defined in (
3.34
). In addition, let
|\Lambda|\geq 1
,
\lambda>0
, and
t>0
, and
N>0
, and assume that
\|f_{0}\|_{X},\|\hat{v}\|_{Y},\|\gamma_{0}\|_{X}<\infty
. Then there exist constants
C>0
dependent on
\|f_{0}\|_{X},\|\gamma_{0}\|_{X},\|\hat{v}\|_{Y},|\Lambda|
, and
K>0
dependent on
\|f_{0}\|_{X},\|\gamma_{0}\|_{X}
s.t. we have for all
J\in L^{2}\cap L^{\infty}({\Lambda^{*}})
that

(2.99)

\displaystyle\Big|\Phi_{t}\,-\,\frac{1}{N^{\frac{3}{2}}}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\ \big(Q_{3}^{(\Phi)}[f](s)+Q_{3,3}^{(\Phi)}[f](s)\big)\Big|\,

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{K\|\hat{v}\|_{Y}|\Lambda|\lambda t}\frac{1}{N^{2}}\,,

(2.100)

\displaystyle\Big|\int\mathrm{d}p\ J(p)\big(f_{t}(p)-f_{0}(p)-\frac{1}{N}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\ Q_{3}[f](s,p)\big)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{K\|\hat{v}\|_{Y}|\Lambda|\lambda t}\frac{\|J\|_{\infty}}{N^{\frac{3}{2}}}\,,

(2.101)

\displaystyle\Big|\int\mathrm{d}p\ J(p)\big(g_{t}(p)-\frac{1}{N}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\ Q_{3}^{(g)}[f](s,p)\big)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{K\|\hat{v}\|_{Y}|\Lambda|\lambda t}\frac{\|J\|_{2}+\|J\|_{\infty}}{N^{\frac{3}{2}}}\,.

Remark 2.9
.

The error bounds in Theorem
2.8
improve those obtained in
[
37
]
significantly. In the latter, the upper bounds were of order
O(\frac{\lambda}{N^{1/2}})
for (
2.99
), and
O(\frac{\lambda}{N})
for (
2.100
), (
2.101
), respectively. The
t
-dependence of the error remained the same in each case. In Theorem
2.8
, the dependence of the error terms with respect to
N
is sharp.

For the following statement, we introduce the mesoscopic fields

(2.102)

\Psi_{T}\,:=\,\Phi_{T/\lambda^{2}},\quad F_{T}\,:=\,f_{T/\lambda^{2}},\quad G_{T}\,:=\,g_{T/\lambda^{2}}\,,

as well as the mesoscopic Boltzmann operators

(2.103)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Q}}^{(\Psi)}_{k}[F](S)\,

\displaystyle:=\,\lambda^{-2}Q^{(\Phi)}_{k}[f](S/\lambda^{2}),\quad{\mathcal{Q}}_{3}[F](S)\,:=\,\lambda^{-2}Q_{3}[f](S/\lambda^{2}),

(2.104)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Q}}_{3}^{(G)}[F](S)\,

\displaystyle:=\,\lambda^{-2}Q_{3}^{(g)}[f](S/\lambda^{2})\,.

Corollary 2.10
.

Under the same assumptions of Theorem
2.8
, for any
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
, there exists a constant
C_{\delta}>0
dependent on
\|f_{0}\|_{X}
,
\|\gamma_{0}\|_{X}
,
\|\hat{v}\|_{Y}
and a constant
K_{\delta}
dependent on
\|f_{0}\|_{X}
,
\|\gamma_{0}\|_{X}
,
\|\hat{v}\|_{Y}
,
|\Lambda|
, such that for
t=\lambda^{-2}T
and
\lambda=\frac{C_{\delta}}{\log N}
, we have that

(2.105)

\displaystyle\Big|\Psi_{T}\,-\,\frac{1}{N^{\frac{3}{2}}}\int_{0}^{T}\mathrm{d}S\ \big({\mathcal{Q}}_{3}^{(\Psi)}[F](S)+{\mathcal{Q}}_{3,3}^{(\Psi)}[F](S)\big)\Big|\,

\displaystyle\leq\frac{K_{\delta}}{N^{\frac{3}{2}+\delta}}\,,

(2.106)

\displaystyle\Big|\int\mathrm{d}p\ J(p)\big(F_{T}(p)-F_{0}(p)-\frac{1}{N}\int_{0}^{T}\mathrm{d}S\ {\mathcal{Q}}_{3}[F](S,p)\big)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{K_{\delta}\|J\|_{\infty}}{N^{1+\delta}}\,,

(2.107)

\displaystyle\Big|\int\mathrm{d}p\ J(p)\big(G_{T}(p)-\frac{1}{N}\int_{0}^{T}\mathrm{d}S\ {\mathcal{Q}}_{3}^{(G)}[F](S,p)\big)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{K_{\delta}(\|J\|_{2}+\|J\|_{\infty})}{N^{1+\delta}}\,.

Remark 2.11
.

Corollary
2.10
improves our previous time window, see
[
37
]
, which was of order
t\sim\big(\frac{\log N}{\log\log N}\big)^{2}
. Here, we obtain
t\sim(\log N)^{2}
.

Remark 2.12
.

In
[
37
]
, we also studied the case
L=\lambda^{-2-}\gg\lambda^{-2}\sim t
. This is due to the reason, that for longer times/shorter system sizes, we observe superposition of waves, while for shorter times, we observe dispersion. In that case, the time window of validity was given by
t\sim\lambda^{-2}
, with
\lambda=O\big(\big(\frac{\log\log N}{\log N}\big)^{\frac{2}{7}-}\big)
. For these times, we observed an
elastic
QBE, for which the dispersion relation is given by the Bogoliubov dispersion
\Omega
. We do not provide such a result in the present work, due to the complicated phase structure in the quantum Boltzmann operators, involving the phases of the HFB fields
(\phi,\sigma)
as well as the
modified
Bogoliubov dispersion
\Omega^{(2)}
, see Remark
2.5
.

2.4.
Sketch of the proof

In Section
3
, we first derive the expansions for the total BEC wave function and total pair correlations. We then compute the perturbation expansion of
f
,
g
and
\Phi
. In that expansion, we collect terms corresponding to the BEC evolution up to order
\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}
.
In particular, they correspond to the first-order Duhamel expansion of
\Phi
and involve a single commutator with
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{fluc}}
.
These terms can be eliminated
, to leading order,
by a suitable choice of the HFB field
\phi

fixing the Weyl transform
{\mathcal{W}}[\sqrt{N|\Lambda|}\phi]
.
The pair-absorption terms in the evolution of
g
up to order
\frac{1}{N}
contain some terms that can be characterized as HFB terms. They arise in the second order Duhamel expansion, either due to a single or double commutator with
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{fluc}}
. These terms, in turn, can be eliminated by a suitable choice of
(\gamma,\sigma)
,
determining the Bogoliubov rotation
{\mathcal{T}}[k_{t}]
, see (
2.13
) and (
2.31
), (
2.27
).
The remaining ’free’ evolution terms can be eliminated by properly choosing the dispersion
\Omega
in
{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathrm{Bog}}(t)
. The
entire
procedure amounts to the renormalization of
(\phi,\gamma,\sigma,\Omega)
. In Section
4
, we derive a priori bounds for the renormalized HFB fields
(\phi,\gamma,\sigma)
. Those allow us to control the tail and other lower-order terms in the Duhamel expansions of
(\Phi,f,g)
.

3.
Derivation of leading order terms

In this section, we sketch the ideas needed to separate the HFB evolution from the QBE evolution. Crucially, we will use the transformation behavior of
a^{\#}
under conjugation with
{\mathcal{W}}
,
{\mathcal{T}}
, and
{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathrm{Bog}}
. In particular, these transformations preserve quasifreeness.

3.1.
Density expansion

In this step, we subtract the HFB dynamics, by way of the unitary transformations associated with
{\mathcal{W}}[\sqrt{N|\Lambda|}\phi_{t}]
,
{\mathcal{T}}[k_{t}]
and
{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathrm{Bog}}(t)
. Notice that for now we are not prescribing the specific dynamics. As we will see below, the dynamics of
(\phi,\gamma,\sigma,\omega)
will emerge from a constraint condition formulated in the leading order dynamics.

(
2.12
) implies

(3.1)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{W}}^{\dagger}[\sqrt{N|\Lambda|}\phi_{t}]a^{\dagger}_{p}a_{p}{\mathcal{W}}[\sqrt{N|\Lambda|}\phi_{t}]

(3.2)

\displaystyle\quad=\,a^{\dagger}_{p}a_{p}\,+\,\delta(p)\Big(\sqrt{N|\Lambda|}(\phi_{t}a^{\dagger}_{0}+\overline{\phi}_{t}a_{0})\,+\,N|\Lambda|^{2}|\phi_{t}|^{2}\Big)\,.

Analogously, (
2.29
), followed by (
2.30
), (
2.31
), yields

(3.3)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\mathcal{T}}^{\dagger}[k_{t}]a^{\dagger}_{p}a_{p}{\mathcal{T}}[k_{t}]\\
&\quad=\,(u_{t}(p)a^{\dagger}_{p}+\overline{v}_{t}(p)a_{-p})(u_{t}(p)a_{p}+v_{t}(p)a^{\dagger}_{-p})\\
&\quad=\,u_{t}(p)^{2}a^{\dagger}_{p}a_{p}+|v_{t}(p)|^{2}a^{\dagger}_{-p}a_{-p}+(u_{t}(p)v_{t}(p)a^{\dagger}_{p}a^{\dagger}_{-p}+\mathrm{h.c.})+|\Lambda||v_{t}(p)|^{2}\\
&\quad=\,(1+\gamma_{t}(p))a^{\dagger}_{p}a_{p}+\gamma_{t}(p)a^{\dagger}_{-p}a_{-p}+(\sigma_{t}(p)a^{\dagger}_{p}a^{\dagger}_{-p}+\mathrm{h.c.})+|\Lambda|\gamma_{t}(p)\,,\end{split}

and, similarly,

(3.4)

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\mathcal{T}}^{\dagger}[k_{t}](\phi_{t}a^{\dagger}_{0}+\overline{\phi}_{t}a_{0}){\mathcal{T}}[k_{t}]\,&=\,\phi_{t}(u_{t}(0)a^{\dagger}_{0}+\overline{v}_{t}(0)a_{0})+\overline{\phi}_{t}(u_{t}(0)a_{0}+v_{t}(0)a^{\dagger}_{0})\\
&=\,\big(\phi_{t}u_{t}(0)+\overline{\phi}_{t}v_{t}(0)\big)a^{\dagger}_{0}+\mathrm{h.c.}\end{split}

Next, (
2.23
) implies

(3.5)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathrm{Bog}}^{\dagger}(t)a_{0}{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathrm{Bog}}(t)\,

\displaystyle=\,e^{-i\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\ \Omega_{s}(0)}a_{0}\,,

(3.6)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathrm{Bog}}^{\dagger}(t)a^{\dagger}_{p}a^{\dagger}_{-p}{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathrm{Bog}}(t)\,

\displaystyle=\,e^{2i\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\ \Omega_{s}(p)}a^{\dagger}_{p}a^{\dagger}_{-p}\,,

while
[a^{\dagger}_{p}a_{p},{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathrm{Bog}}(t)]=0
.

Collecting (
3.1
), (
3.3
), (
3.4
), (
3.5
) and (
3.6
), and using the fact that
f_{t}
is even, we can rewrite the total density in terms of the relative densities

(3.7)

\displaystyle f^{(\mathrm{tot})}_{t}(p)\,=

\displaystyle\,\delta(p)\Big[N|\Lambda||\phi_{t}|^{2}\,+\,\Big(\sqrt{N|\Lambda|}e^{i\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\ \Omega_{s}(0)}\big(\phi_{t}u_{t}(0)+\overline{\phi}_{t}v_{t}(0)\big)\overline{\Phi}_{t}\,+\,\mathrm{h.c.}\Big)\Big]

(3.8)

\displaystyle\,+\,\gamma_{t}(p)\,+\,\big(1+\gamma_{t}(p)\big)f_{t}(p)\,+\,\gamma_{t}(p)f_{t}(-p)\,+\,\Big(e^{2i\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\ \Omega_{s}(p)}\sigma_{t}(p)\overline{g}_{t}(p)+\mathrm{h.c.}\Big)\,.

Similarly, we have that

(3.9)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{W}}^{\dagger}[\sqrt{N|\Lambda|}\phi_{t}]a_{p}a_{-p}{\mathcal{W}}[\sqrt{N|\Lambda|}\phi_{t}]\,=\,a_{p}a_{-p}\,+\,\delta(p)\Big(2\sqrt{N|\Lambda|}\phi_{t}a_{0}\,+\,N|\Lambda|^{2}\phi_{t}^{2}\Big)\,,

and that

(3.10)

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\mathcal{T}}^{\dagger}[k_{t}]a_{p}a_{-p}{\mathcal{T}}[k_{t}]\,=&\,(u_{t}(p)a_{p}+v_{t}(p)a^{\dagger}_{-p})(u_{t}(p)a_{-p}+v_{t}(p)a^{\dagger}_{p})\\
=&\,|\Lambda|\sigma_{t}(p)\,+\,\sigma_{t}(a^{\dagger}_{p}a_{p}+a^{\dagger}_{-p}a_{-p})\,+\,(1+\gamma_{t}(p))a_{p}a_{-p}\\
&\,+\,\frac{\sigma_{t}(p)^{2}}{1+\gamma_{t}(p)}a^{\dagger}_{p}a^{\dagger}_{-p}\,.\end{split}

Following analogous steps as above, we obtain

(3.11)

\displaystyle g^{(\mathrm{tot})}_{t}(p)\,=

\displaystyle\,\delta(p)\Big[N|\Lambda|\phi_{t}^{2}\,+\,2\sqrt{N|\Lambda|}\phi_{t}(u_{t}(0)e^{-i\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\ \Omega_{s}(0)}\Phi_{t}+v_{t}(0)e^{i\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\ \Omega_{s}(0)}\overline{\Phi}_{t})\Big]

(3.12)

\displaystyle\,+\,\sigma_{t}(p)\,+\,\sigma_{t}(f_{t}(p)+f_{t}(-p))\,+\,(1+\gamma_{t}(p))e^{2i\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\ \Omega_{s}(p)}g_{t}(p)

(3.13)

\displaystyle\,+\,\frac{\sigma_{t}(p)^{2}e^{-2i\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\ \Omega_{s}(p)}\overline{g}_{t}(p)}{1+\gamma_{t}(p)}\,,

and also

(3.14)

\displaystyle\Phi^{(\mathrm{tot})}_{t}\,=\,\sqrt{N|\Lambda|}\phi_{t}\,+\,u_{t}(0)e^{-i\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}\tau\ \Omega_{\tau}(0)}\Phi_{t}\,+\,v_{t}(0)e^{i\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}\tau\ \Omega_{\tau}(0)}\overline{\Phi}\,.

3.2.
Perturbation expansion

Notice that thus far, we have not specified the fields
(\phi,\gamma,\sigma,\Omega)
involved in the subtracted dynamics in the previous subsection. As we will see, the dynamics will be fixed by eliminating non-QBE terms in the leading order of the fluctuation dynamics. Consequently, the remaining terms in the fluctuation dynamics will describe the QBE to leading order.

We are interested in the evolution of

(3.15)

\displaystyle f_{t}(p)\,=\,\frac{\langle{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathrm{fluc}}^{\dagger}(t)a^{\dagger}_{p}a_{p}{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathrm{fluc}}(t)\rangle_{0}}{|\Lambda|}\,.

Note that due to conjugation with
{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathrm{fluc}}(t)
, the phase-factor
e^{iS_{t}}
in
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{fluc}}(t)
drops out. Using the Duhamel expansion, we obtain that

(3.16)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f_{t}(p)-f_{0}(p)\,=\,}

\displaystyle-i\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\ \frac{\langle[a^{\dagger}_{p}a_{p},{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{fluc}}(s)]\rangle_{0}}{|\Lambda|}

(3.17)

\displaystyle-\,\int_{[0,t]^{2}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{s}_{2}\ \mathds{1}_{s_{1}\geq s_{2}}\,\frac{\langle[[a^{\dagger}_{p}a_{p},{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{fluc}}(s_{1})],{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{fluc}}(s_{2})]\rangle_{0}}{|\Lambda|}

(3.18)

\displaystyle+\,\int_{[0,t]^{3}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{s}_{3}\ \frac{\langle[[[a^{\dagger}_{p}a_{p},{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{fluc}}(s_{1})],{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{fluc}}(s_{2})],{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{fluc}}(s_{3})]\rangle_{s_{3}}}{|\Lambda|}\,.

Translation invariance and gauge invariance imply that

(3.19)

\displaystyle\langle[a^{\dagger}_{p}a_{p},{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{fluc}}(s)]\rangle_{0}\,=\,[\hbox to9.91pt{\vbox to11.85pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\enskip\lower-5.92555pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

Next, we have, due to gauge invariance, that

(3.20)

\displaystyle\frac{\langle[[a^{\dagger}_{p}a_{p},{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{fluc}}(s_{1})],{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{fluc}}(s_{2})]\rangle_{0}}{|\Lambda|}

(3.21)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\langle[[a^{\dagger}_{p}a_{p},{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{BEC}}(s_{1})+{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{cub}}(s_{1})],{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{BEC}}(s_{2})+{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{cub}}(s_{2})]\rangle_{0}}{|\Lambda|}

(3.22)

\displaystyle+\,\frac{\langle[[a^{\dagger}_{p}a_{p},{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{HFB}}(s_{1})+{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{quart}}(s_{1})],{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{HFB}}(s_{2})+{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{quart}}(s_{2})]\rangle_{0}}{|\Lambda|}\,.

3.22
)
not
corresponding to the quantum Boltzmann dynamics, which we will obtain from the contraction

We will next describe how to gradually eliminate all other terms.

3.2.1.
First order HFB renormalization

The following analysis is analogous to the approach studied in
[
60
]
and establishes the same equations.

For general fields
(\phi,\gamma,\sigma)
, the leading order contributions in (
3.22
) are generated by
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{BEC}}
and
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{HFB}}
. Thus, a possible choice is to set

(3.24)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{BEC}}(t)\,=

\displaystyle\,0\,,

(3.25)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{HFB}}(t)\,=

\displaystyle\,0\,.

Observe that it is sufficient for calculating the leading order expressions to assume

(3.26)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{BEC}}(t),{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{HFB}}(t)=O(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}})\,.

Lemma
A.2
implies that (
3.24
) is equivalent to

(3.27)

\displaystyle u_{t}(0)\Big(-i\partial_{t}\phi_{t}+\lambda|\Lambda||\phi_{t}|^{2}\hat{v}(0)\phi_{t}+\frac{\lambda}{N}\int\mathrm{d}p\ \hat{v}(p)\sigma_{t}(p)\overline{\phi}_{t}

(3.28)

\displaystyle+\,\frac{\lambda}{N}\int\mathrm{d}p\ \big(\hat{v}(p)+\hat{v}(0)\big)\gamma_{t}(p)\phi_{t}\Big)\,+\,v_{t}(0)\Big(-\overline{i\partial_{t}\phi_{t}}+\lambda|\Lambda||\phi_{t}|^{2}\hat{v}(0)\overline{\phi}_{t}

(3.29)

\displaystyle+\frac{\lambda}{N}\int\mathrm{d}p\ \hat{v}(p)\overline{\sigma}_{t}(p)\phi_{t}+\frac{\lambda}{N}\int\mathrm{d}p\ \big(\hat{v}(p)+\hat{v}(0)\big)\gamma_{t}(p)\overline{\phi}_{t}\Big)\,=\,0\,.

Abbreviating

(3.30)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{(1)}_{t}(p):=

\displaystyle\gamma_{t}(p)\,+\,N|\Lambda||\phi_{t}|^{2}\delta(p)\,,

(3.31)

\displaystyle\Sigma^{(1)}_{t}(p):=

\displaystyle\sigma_{t}(p)\,+\,N|\Lambda|\phi_{t}^{2}\delta(p)\,,

this condition is satisfied if

(3.32)

\displaystyle\begin{split}i\partial_{t}\phi^{(1)}_{t}\,=&\,\frac{\lambda}{N}\Big(\big(\Gamma^{(1)}_{t}*(\hat{v}+\hat{v}(0))\big)(0)\phi^{(1)}_{t}\,+\,(\Sigma^{(1)}_{t}*\hat{v})(0)\overline{\phi}^{(1)}_{t}\Big)\\
&-\,2\lambda|\Lambda|\hat{v}(0)|\phi^{(1)}_{t}|^{2}\phi^{(1)}_{t}\,.\end{split}

The superscript ’
(1)
’ accounts for renormalization to first order, as detailed below. For now, they do not play a specific role.

Since
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{HFB}}^{\mathrm{(d)}}(t)
is a diagonal quadratic operator, we can absorb it into phase factors in
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{BEC}}(t)
,
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{cub}}(t)
, and
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{quart}}(t)
. For that purpose, we set

(3.33)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{HFB}}^{\mathrm{(d)}}(t)\,=\,0\,,

or equivalently,

(3.34)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\Omega^{(1)}_{t}(p)\,=\,\\
&\Big(E(p)+\frac{\lambda}{N}\big((\gamma^{(1)}_{t}+N|\Lambda||\phi^{(1)}_{t}|^{2}\delta)*(\hat{v}+\hat{v}(0))\big)(p)\Big)\big(1+2\gamma^{(1)}_{t}(p)\big)\\
&+\,\frac{2\lambda}{N}\operatorname{Re}\Big(\big((\overline{\sigma}^{(1)}_{t}+N|\Lambda|(\overline{\phi}^{(1)}_{t})^{2}\delta)*\hat{v}\big)(p)\sigma^{(1)}_{t}(p)\Big)\,-\frac{\operatorname{Re}\big(\overline{\sigma}^{(1)}_{t}(p)i\partial_{t}\sigma^{(1)}_{t}(p)\big)}{1+\gamma^{(1)}_{t}(p)}\,.\end{split}

see Lemma
A.5
.

Thus (
3.25
) is satisfied if

(3.35)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{HFB}}^{\mathrm{(cor)}}(t)\,=\,0\,,

which has been elaborated on in
[
57
,
58
,
59
]
. Lemma
A.2
implies that (
3.35
) is satisfied if

(3.36)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\frac{i\partial_{t}\sigma^{(1)}_{t}(p)}{2}\,-\,\frac{\sigma^{(1)}_{t}(p)i\partial_{t}\gamma^{(1)}_{t}(p)}{2(1+\gamma^{(1)}_{t}(p))}\,=\,\\
&\Big(E(p)+\frac{\lambda}{N}\Gamma^{(1)}_{t}*\big(\hat{v}+\hat{v}(0)\big)(p)\Big)\sigma^{(1)}_{t}(p)\\
&+\,\frac{\lambda}{2N}\Big(\big(\Sigma^{(1)}_{t}*\hat{v}\big)(p)(1+\gamma^{(1)}_{t}(p))\,+\,\big(\overline{\Sigma}^{(1)}_{t}*\hat{v}\big)(p)\frac{\sigma^{(1)}_{t}(p)^{2}}{1+\gamma^{(1)}_{t}(p)}\Big)\,.\end{split}

We show in Lemma
A.4
that (
3.36
) is equivalent to

(3.37)

\displaystyle i\partial_{t}\gamma^{(1)}_{t}\,

\displaystyle=\,\frac{\lambda}{N}\big[\big(\Sigma^{(1)}_{t}*\hat{v}\big)\overline{\sigma}^{(1)}_{t}\,-\,\big(\overline{\Sigma}^{(1)}_{t}*\hat{v}\big)\,\sigma^{(1)}_{t}\big]\,,

(3.38)

\displaystyle i\partial_{t}\sigma^{(1)}_{t}\,

\displaystyle=\,2\big(E+\frac{\lambda}{N}\Gamma^{(1)}_{t}*(\hat{v}+\hat{v}(0))\big)\sigma^{(1)}_{t}\,+\,\frac{\lambda}{N}\big(\Sigma^{(1)}_{t}*\hat{v}\big)(1+2\gamma^{(1)}_{t})\,.

Together with (
3.32
), we thus have shown that

(3.39)

\displaystyle\begin{split}i\partial_{t}\phi^{(1)}_{t}\,=&\,\frac{\lambda}{N}\Big(\big(\Gamma^{(1)}_{t}*(\hat{v}+\hat{v}(0))\big)(0)\phi^{(1)}_{t}\,+\,(\Sigma^{(1)}_{t}*\hat{v})(0)\overline{\phi}^{(1)}_{t}\Big)\\
&-\,2\lambda|\Lambda|\hat{v}(0)|\phi^{(1)}_{t}|^{2}\phi^{(1)}_{t}\,.\\
\partial_{t}\gamma^{(1)}_{t}\,=&\,\frac{2\lambda}{N}\operatorname{Im}\big((\Sigma^{(1)}_{t}*\hat{v})\overline{\sigma}^{(1)}_{t}\big)\,,\\
i\partial_{t}\sigma^{(1)}_{t}\,=&\,2\big(E+\frac{\lambda}{N}\Gamma^{(1)}_{t}*(\hat{v}+\hat{v}(0))\big)\sigma^{(1)}_{t}\,+\,\frac{\lambda}{N}\big(\Sigma^{(1)}_{t}*\hat{v}\big)(1+2\gamma^{(1)}_{t})\,.\end{split}

These are the well-known HFB equations in the translation invariant case, see, e.g.,
[
8
,
57
]
. Moreover, Lemma
A.5
implies that, if
\sigma^{(1)}
satisfies (
3.39
), then the Bogoliubov dispersion, see (
3.34
), satisfies

(3.40)

\displaystyle\Omega^{(1)}_{t}\,=\,E+\frac{\lambda}{N}\Gamma^{(1)}_{t}*\big(\hat{v}+\hat{v}(0)\big)\,+\,\frac{\lambda}{N}\frac{\operatorname{Re}\big((\Sigma^{(1)}_{t}*\hat{v})\overline{\sigma}^{(1)}_{t}\big)}{1+\gamma^{(1)}_{t}}\,.

3.2.2.
Second order HFB renormalization

We now perform a recursion that extends the previous construction in a natural sense. Namely, eliminating terms only in the first-order Duhamel expansion does not ensure that corrections to the quantum Boltzmann evolution in the second-order Duhamel expansion vanish. Accordingly, we impose corrections to the equations for the governing the HFB fields, to eliminate those corrections.

In order to determine the cubic Boltzmann operator, we follow
[
37
]
and compute the second order Duhamel expansion.

Observe that for a self-adjoint operator
A
, operators
B
,
C
and any state
\nu
we have that

(3.41)

\displaystyle\nu([[A,B+B^{\dagger}],C+C^{\dagger}])\,=\,2\operatorname{Re}\big(\nu([[A,B],C])+\nu([[A,B],C^{\dagger}])\big)\,.

Remark 3.1

(Commutator rule)
.

We note that, due to the commutators, every right argument in a commutator needs to be
contracted with
at least one argument to the left of it. We refer to this fact as the
commutator rule
.

Remark 3.2
.

Observe that we have that

(3.42)

\displaystyle 2\operatorname{Re}\int_{[0,t]^{2}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{s}_{2}\ \mathds{1}_{s_{1}\geq s_{2}}A(s_{1})\overline{A}(s_{2})\,=\,\Big|\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\ A(s)\Big|^{2}\,.

(3.46)

\displaystyle=

(3.47)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta(p)\Big|-i\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\ \frac{\langle[a_{0},{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{cub}}(s)]\rangle_{0}}{|\Lambda|}\Big|^{2}\,,

see Remark
3.2
. As we show in

, we have that this condensate contribution of size
\frac{\lambda^{2}t^{2}}{N}

(3.49)

\displaystyle+\,\frac{\langle[[\hbox to9.45pt{\vbox to8.99pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.72627pt\lower-4.49501pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

&=\,\delta(p)\Big|-i\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\ \frac{\langle[a_{0},{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{BEC}}(s)\,+\,{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{cub}}(s)]\rangle_{0}}{|\Lambda|}\Big|^{2}\,.\end{split}

In order to eliminate this contribution, we choose

(3.51)

\displaystyle\langle[a_{0},{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{BEC}}(t)+{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{cub}}(t)]\rangle_{0}\,=\,0\,.

This condition is equivalent to eliminating all first-order terms in the second-order Duhamel expansion of
\Phi
.

Lemma
A.2
implies that

(3.52)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\frac{\langle[a_{0},{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{BEC}}(t)]\rangle_{0}}{|\Lambda|}\,=\\
&\sqrt{N|\Lambda|}\Big[u_{t}(0)\Big(-i\partial_{t}\phi_{t}+\lambda|\Lambda||\phi_{t}|^{2}\hat{v}(0)\phi_{t}+\frac{\lambda}{N}\int\mathrm{d}p\ \hat{v}(p)\sigma_{t}(p)\overline{\phi}_{t}\\
&+\,\frac{\lambda}{N}\int\mathrm{d}p\ \big(\hat{v}(p)+\hat{v}(0)\big)\gamma_{t}(p)\phi_{t}\Big)\,+\,v_{t}(0)\Big(-\overline{i\partial_{t}\phi_{t}}+\lambda|\Lambda||\phi_{t}|^{2}\hat{v}(0)\overline{\phi}_{t}\\
&+\frac{\lambda}{N}\int\mathrm{d}p\ \hat{v}(p)\overline{\sigma}_{t}(p)\phi_{t}+\frac{\lambda}{N}\int\mathrm{d}p\ \big(\hat{v}(p)+\hat{v}(0)\big)\gamma_{t}(p)\overline{\phi}_{t}\Big)\Big]e^{i\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\ \Omega_{s}(0)}\,,\end{split}

and (
3.43
) yields

(3.53)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\frac{\langle[a_{0},{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{cub}}(t)]\rangle_{0}}{|\Lambda|}\,=\,\frac{\langle[a_{0},\hskip 6.7284pt\hbox to20.39pt{\vbox to1pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 10.19447pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

&2\sigma_{t}(p)\overline{\phi}_{t}\hat{v}(p)\Big)f_{0}(p)+v_{t}(0)\int\mathrm{d}p\ \Big(\big(1+2\gamma_{t}(p)\big)\overline{\phi}_{t}\big(\hat{v}(p)+\hat{v}(0)\big)+\\
&2\overline{\sigma}_{t}(p)\phi_{t}\hat{v}(p)\Big)f_{0}(p)\,.\end{split}

In order to satisfy (
3.51
), it suffices to equate the sum of the coefficients of
u_{t}(0)
in (
3.52
) and (
3.53
) to zero. This condition is equivalent to

(3.54)

\displaystyle\begin{split}i\partial_{t}\phi_{t}\,=&\,\lambda|\Lambda||\phi_{t}|^{2}\hat{v}(0)\phi_{t}+\frac{\lambda}{N}\int\mathrm{d}p\ \hat{v}(p)\sigma_{t}(p)\overline{\phi}_{t}\,+\,\frac{\lambda}{N}\int\mathrm{d}p\ \big(\hat{v}(p)+\hat{v}(0)\big)\gamma_{t}(p)\phi_{t}\\
&+\,\frac{\lambda}{N}\int\mathrm{d}p\ \Big(\big(1+2\gamma_{t}(p)\big)\phi_{t}\big(\hat{v}(p)+\hat{v}(0)\big)+2\sigma_{t}(p)\overline{\phi}_{t}\hat{v}(p)\Big)f_{0}(p)\,.\end{split}

Observe that all integrands except for
f_{0}
are even
in
p
. In anticipation of the evolution of
\gamma
and
\sigma
, recall from (
2.38
)

(3.55)

\displaystyle f_{0}^{(+)}(p)\,=\,\frac{1}{2}\big(f_{0}(p)+f_{0}(-p)\big)\,,

the even symmetrization of
f_{0}
. In analogy to (
3.30
), (
3.31
), we introduce the second order renormalized shifted expectations

(3.56)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{(2)}:=

\displaystyle(1+2f_{0}^{(+)})\gamma\,+\,f_{0}^{(+)}\,+\,N|\Lambda||\phi|^{2}\delta\,,

(3.57)

\displaystyle\Sigma^{(2)}:=

\displaystyle(1+2f_{0}^{(+)})\sigma\,+\,N|\Lambda|\phi^{2}\delta\,.

With these, and writing
\phi=\phi^{(2)}
, we can simplify (
3.54
) into

(3.58)

\displaystyle\begin{split}i\partial_{t}\phi^{(2)}_{t}\,=&\,\frac{\lambda}{N}\Big(\big(\Gamma^{(2)}_{t}*(\hat{v}+\hat{v}(0))\big)(0)\phi^{(2)}_{t}\,+\,(\Sigma^{(2)}_{t}*\hat{v})(0)\overline{\phi}^{(2)}_{t}\Big)\\
&-\,2\lambda|\Lambda|\hat{v}(0)|\phi^{(2)}_{t}|^{2}\phi^{(2)}_{t}\,.\end{split}

We recognize that (
3.58
) is a renormalization of (
3.32
), where we substituted
(\Gamma^{(1)},\Sigma^{(1)})
by the renormalized fields
(\Gamma^{(2)},\Sigma^{(2)})
.

Next, observe that

(3.59)

\displaystyle[a^{\dagger}_{p}a_{p},{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{HFB}}^{\mathrm{(cor)}}(t)]\,=\,A_{t}(p)a_{p}a_{-p}\,+\mathrm{h.c.}

for some coefficient
A_{t}
. In particular, (
3.41
) yields

(3.60)

\displaystyle\langle[[a^{\dagger}_{p}a_{p},{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{HFB}}^{\mathrm{(cor)}}(s_{1})],{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{HFB}}^{\mathrm{(cor)}}(s_{2})+{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{quart}}(s_{2})]\rangle_{0}

(3.61)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\operatorname{Re}\big(A_{s_{1}}(p)\langle[a_{p}a_{-p},{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{HFB}}^{\mathrm{(cor)}}(s_{2})+{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{quart}}(s_{2})]\rangle_{0}\big)\,.
